\begin{center}{\LARGE\sffamily\bfseries\color{heading}Lower bounds for quadratic and cubic\\[.08em]polynomial values at \(\pi\)\par}\vspace{.6em}{\large An expository edition for advanced undergraduates\par}\vspace{.35em}{\small 19 November 2026\par}\end{center}
\textbf{Abstract.} Let \(H(P)\) be the largest absolute coefficient of a nonzero integer polynomial. For \(\deg P\le d\) we prove \(|P(\pi)|\ge c_d H(P)^{-w_d}\), with \(w_2=16.1942\) and \(w_3=27.4188\). The constants \(c_d>0\) are independent of \(P\). A nonvanishing interpolation determinant supplies one Gaussian-integer resultant. Separate analytic paths for its rows bound the contribution of a root near \(\pi\), while estimates at the other conjugates recover the polynomial-value exponent. The numerical margins are certified by finite rational inequalities. A separate rectangular construction gives an asymptotic coefficient \(8.32\) for sufficiently large degrees.

\textbf{Keywords.} Transcendence measure, interpolation determinant, resultant, logarithmic energy, approximation of \(\pi\).

\textbf{How to read this paper.} The prerequisites are undergraduate analysis, linear algebra, elementary number theory, abstract algebra and basic complex analysis. Start with Section 1.2, read the construction of the integer in \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)}, and then see how Section 7 forces its absolute value below one. Sections 3--6 give the intermediate estimates. Appendix C proves the two background theorems used in the argument.

\medskip\tableofcontents\clearpage
\section{1. Statement and relation to earlier work}\label{statement-and-relation-to-earlier-work}

For an integer polynomial there are two separate questions. Can its value at \(\pi\) be zero? If the value is nonzero, how small can it be compared with the size of the coefficients? Transcendence answers the first question. The powers of the height in Theorem 1 quantify the second.

\subsection{1.1. Reading the statement}\label{reading-the-statement}

Write \(\mathcal L(P)=\sum_j |p_j|\) and \(H(P)=\max_j |p_j|\) for \(P(Y)=\sum_j p_jY^j\). We use natural logarithms. Define \(\omega_d(\xi)\) as the supremum of the real \(w\) for which \(0<|P(\xi)|<H(P)^{-w}\) for infinitely many distinct nonzero \(P\in\mathbb Z[Y]\) of degree at most \(d\). Only finitely many such polynomials have bounded height. When the auxiliary polynomial has complex coefficients, its length uses the same sum with the complex modulus on each coefficient.

\textbf{Theorem 1.} For each \(d\in\{2,3\}\) there exists \(c_d>0\) such that, for every nonzero \(P\in\mathbb Z[Y]\) with \(\deg P\le d\),

\[
 |P(\pi)|\ \ge\ c_d H(P)^{-w_d},\qquad
 w_2=\frac{80971}{5000}=16.1942,\quad
 w_3=\frac{68547}{2500}=27.4188.                                      \tag{1}\label{eq:1}
\]

Consequently \(\omega_2(\pi)\le16.1942\) and \(\omega_3(\pi)\le27.4188\). Indeed, for \(w>w_d\), \(c_dH^{-w_d}>H^{-w}\) for all sufficiently large \(H\). The endpoint in \hyperref[eq:1]{(1)} itself holds without an additional \(\varepsilon\). This includes nonprimitive and reducible polynomials, repeated factors and constants. The proof gives existence of \(c_d\) without a numerical value or starting height.

For example, \(P(Y)=7Y^2-22Y+1\) has height \(22\) and length \(30\). Both are measures of the polynomial before evaluation. For height at most \(H\) and degree at most \(d\) there are at most \((2\lfloor H\rfloor+1)^{d+1}\) coefficient vectors. Hence an assertion about infinitely many distinct polynomials is an assertion about unbounded height. By contrast, a single extremely small value at a fixed height can affect \(c_d\). To obtain the endpoint one must prove that the infimum of \(H(P)^{w_d}|P(\pi)|\) is positive. An upper bound on the supremum defining \(\omega_d\) alone would allow this infimum to vanish.

For a separate range of degrees, Appendix B proves

\[
 \omega_d(\pi)\le \frac{208}{25}d\log d+d-2\quad(d\ \text{sufficiently large}),\qquad
 \limsup_{d\to\infty}\frac{\omega_d(\pi)}{d\log d}\le 8.32.              \tag{2}\label{eq:2}
\]

In \hyperref[eq:2]{(2)} the height tends to infinity for each fixed degree before the degree tends to infinity. The threshold on \(d\) is unspecified. A bound \(\omega_d(\pi)=o(d\log d)\) would require the normalized exponent to tend to zero and remains an open direction here.

Nesterenko and Waldschmidt give an explicit general-degree bound and a multiplicity estimate for exponential interpolation. With \(\mathcal L(P)\le L_1\), \(L_1\ge3\), their polynomial-value estimate reads \(\log|P(\pi)|\ge-2\cdot10^6d(\log L_1+d\log d)(1+\log d)\) {[}1, Theorem 2(2) and Lemma 2{]}. For each fixed \(d\), the coefficient of \(\log L_1\) in the negative exponent gives a height exponent \(2\cdot10^6d(1+\log d)\). Alexencev studied approximation of \(\pi\) by algebraic numbers with explicit dependence on degree and height {[}2{]}. These are related problems because a small value of \(P(\pi)\) involves the distances to all of its roots. The distinction already appears for \(P(Y)=qY^3-p\), which tests a particular rational approximation to \(\pi^3\). Theorem 1 allows all four coefficients to vary. Its proof below is independent of any irrationality measure for a power of \(\pi\).

Sections 2--4 construct the nonzero integer and control its algebraic size. Sections 5--6 bound the determinant near \(\pi\). Section 7 combines these estimates over all conjugates. Section 8 and Appendix A give the data and the finite verification. All limiting estimates in Sections 2--8 fix the indicated shape before the height tends to infinity.

\subsection{1.2. Overview of the proof strategy}\label{overview-of-the-proof-strategy}

Read the right-hand side of \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)} first. A polynomial \(f\) with integer coefficients has conjugate roots \(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_e\). An auxiliary polynomial \(R\) with Gaussian-integer coefficients gives a product of the numbers \(R(\alpha_i)\), multiplied by a power of the leading coefficient of \(f\). This product, up to sign, is a determinant called a \emph{resultant}. If \(R(\alpha_1)\ne0\) and \(f\) remains irreducible over the Gaussian rationals, that determinant belongs to \(\mathbb Z[i]\setminus\{0\}\). Its absolute value is at least one. We will bound exactly this product from above.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Suppose a sequence of irreducibles makes \(H(f)^{w_d}|f(\pi)|\) tend to zero. The root factorization converts this into a total required closeness \(\sum_i c_i\ge w_d+1\), measured on the scale \(X=\log\mathcal L(f)\). An integer discriminant limits how much closeness can be shared among roots. At these exponents the closest root must be real.
\item
  Fix that root and select a square matrix whose determinant is nonzero \textbf{at that root}. Each integer frequency \(t\) and node \(v\) has \(e^{(i\pi/2)vt}=i^{vt}\). Four residue classes therefore suffice to record an exponential exactly in the coefficient field. The same identity will later allow us to continue the entries as analytic functions. The selection proof needs only the phases and \(\alpha_1\ne0\).
\item
  Factor a forced power of \(Y\) out of the determinant and clear rational denominators. This gives the particular polynomial \(R\) used in every factor of \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)}. Degree control pays for the leading coefficient of \(f\). A bound on the length of \(R\) controls conjugates far from \(\pi\).
\item
  At a nearby root, expand the determinant in powers of \(\alpha_i-\pi\). Within each term a high-order zero of an analytic function improves the bound. The estimates can use different paths at different nodes and different parameters at different roots, because they estimate values of the already chosen polynomial.
\item
  Add the logarithms in \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)}. The strict margins in Table 2 make the upper bound negative after division by \(nX\), whereas the lower bound is zero on this scale. Factorization then gives the statement for arbitrary \(P\).
\end{enumerate}

One can read Sections 2, 6.1 and 7 first to follow the contradiction, keeping \hyperref[eq:8]{(8)}, \hyperref[eq:9]{(9)}, \hyperref[eq:13]{(13)} and \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)} as intermediate estimates. Sections 3--6 prove these estimates. Appendix A shows how to verify them on whole intervals. Appendix B starts a separate proof with a rectangular matrix. Appendix C supplies two background theorems used in the text. The numerical witnesses and a replay are included at the end for readers who wish to check the finite arithmetic.
