\section{2. Heights, conjugate roots and a nonzero integer}\label{heights-conjugate-roots-and-a-nonzero-integer}

A root of very large modulus contributes a large factor to \(f(\pi)\) even if it carries little information about approximation to \(\pi\). We first divide every distance by its natural root size. A second integer, the discriminant, measures how tightly several roots can cluster. These two calculations explain both the extra \(1\) in the root threshold and the special role of one real root.

\textbf{Nonvanishing for a fixed input.} We use the fact that \(\pi\) is transcendental, meaning that it is a zero of no nonzero polynomial over \(\mathbb Q\). An integral and a divisibility argument suffice to prove this from \(1+e^{i\pi}=0\). Appendix C.1 gives the proof. This fact is already available for fixed inputs when we take logarithms or discard finitely many heights. The quantitative argument below has the separate task of controlling a sequence of different inputs.

Let \(f(Y)=a\prod_{i=1}^{e}(Y-\alpha_i)\) be primitive and irreducible over \(\mathbb Q\), with \(1\le e\le d\). Primitive means that the coefficients have greatest common divisor one. Set \(B_i=\max(1,|\alpha_i|)\) and \(\mathcal M(f)=|a|\prod_i B_i\). The latter quantity, the multiplicative Mahler measure, is multiplicative by the root factorization. We shall use

\[
 H(f)\le \mathcal L(f)\le 2^e\mathcal M(f),\qquad
 \mathcal M(f)\le\mathcal L(f)\le(e+1)H(f).                             \tag{3}\label{eq:3}
\]

Expanding the product term by term gives \(\mathcal L(f)\le |a|\prod_i(1+|\alpha_i|)\). For the other direction we need the polynomial case of \textbf{Jensen's formula}, which identifies a logarithmic mean on the unit circle with a sum over the roots.

\textbf{Jensen's formula, including a root on the contour.} Write \(\langle g\rangle=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}g(t)\,dt\). For \(|\alpha|<1\) the series \(-\sum_{k\ge1}\alpha^ke^{-ikt}/k\) converges absolutely and uniformly, and its real part is \(\log|1-\alpha e^{-it}|\). Each nonconstant exponential integrates to zero. For \(|\alpha|>1\) write \(|e^{it}-\alpha|=|\alpha||1-e^{it}/\alpha|\). The second factor has logarithmic mean zero by the same uniform series with positive frequencies, giving \(\log|\alpha|\). If \(|\alpha|=1\), rotate the variable to put the root at \(1\). For \(1/2\le r<1\) we have \[
 2\sin^2(t/2)\le |e^{it}-r|^2=(1-r)^2+4r\sin^2(t/2)\le4 .
\] On \([-\pi,\pi]\) the inequality \(|\sin(t/2)|\ge |t|/\pi\) bounds the negative logarithm by a constant plus \(|\log |t||\), integrable near zero because \(\int_0^1-\log t\,dt=1\). Dominated convergence gives mean zero at \(r=1\). Summing means in the factorization gives \(\langle\log |f|\rangle=\log\mathcal M(f)\). Exponentiating the bound by \(\log\mathcal L(f)\) gives the second half of \hyperref[eq:3]{(3)}. The other half uses the triangle inequality for each coefficient of the expanded product and \(1+|\alpha_i|\le2B_i\).

Put \(X=\log\mathcal L(f)\). On a sequence with \(X\to\infty\), pass to a subsequence of fixed degree. Order the roots so that \(u_i=X^{-1}\log^+(B_i/|\pi-\alpha_i|)\) decreases, where \(\log^+x=\max(0,\log x)\). Pass again so that \(u_i\to c_i\) in \([0,+\infty]\). Then

\[
 \lim \frac{-\log|f(\pi)|}{X}=\sum_{i=1}^e c_i-1,\qquad
 \sum_{i=2}^e(i-1)c_i\le e-1.                                          \tag{4}\label{eq:4}
\]

For the first equality, \(\log\mathcal M(f)=X+O_d(1)\) by \hyperref[eq:3]{(3)}, and \(|\pi-\alpha_i|/B_i\le1+\pi\) bounds the cost of truncating the logarithm. The discriminant of \(f\) is a nonzero integer, as follows from its symmetric root formula (or the determinant formula for the discriminant) and separability in characteristic zero. Its square root in absolute value is

\[
 1\le |\operatorname{disc} f|^{1/2}
   =\mathcal M(f)^{e-1}\prod_{i<j}\frac{|\alpha_i-\alpha_j|}{B_iB_j}.
\]

The triangle inequality about \(\pi\) shows that each normalized difference is at most \(2(1+\pi)\exp(-Xu_j)\) for \(i<j\). Taking logarithms proves \hyperref[eq:4]{(4)}. In degrees two and three the second inequality reads \(c_2\le1\) and \(c_2+2c_3\le2\), respectively, for an irreducible polynomial of that exact degree. In particular \(y=\sum_{i>1}c_i\le e-1\). If the first root is nonreal its conjugate has the same normalized distance, so \(c_1=c_2\) and \(\sum_i c_i-1\le 2e-3\). Subsequences may be chosen with a fixed real/nonreal alternative. For a violation of \hyperref[eq:1]{(1)} the first root must therefore be real and tend to \(\pi\). Even \(c_1=+\infty\) can be tested below with a finite lower bound for \(c_1\).

Here is the calculation behind the two limits. The ratio \(r_i=|\pi-\alpha_i|/B_i\) lies in \((0,1+\pi]\). Thus \(\log r_i=-Xu_i+\max(0,\log r_i)\) with the last term bounded independently of the root. The logarithm of the root product is \(\log\mathcal M(f)+\sum_i\log r_i\) and \(\log\mathcal M(f)=X+O_d(1)\). Because \(e\) is fixed we can pass to limits even if the largest \(c_i\) is infinite. Subsequence extraction uses compactness of \([0,\infty]\), for instance via \(u\mapsto u/(1+u)\).

For the discriminant bound, \(r_i\le(1+\pi)e^{-Xu_i}\) and \(B_i\ge1\). Hence \[
 \frac{|\alpha_i-\alpha_j|}{B_iB_j}
 \le r_i/B_j+r_j/B_i\le2(1+\pi)e^{-Xu_j}\quad(i<j).
\] The index \(j\) appears \(j-1\) times. Divide the logarithmic inequality by \(X\) to obtain \hyperref[eq:4]{(4)}. An irreducible in characteristic zero has distinct roots because its derivative has smaller degree and is relatively prime to it. Integrality of the discriminant follows concretely from the Sylvester determinant below. For generic coefficients its value for \((f,f')\) is, up to sign, \(a\) times \(a^{2e-2}\prod_{i<j}(\alpha_i-\alpha_j)^2\), by substituting \(f'(\alpha_i)=a\prod_{j\ne i}(\alpha_i-\alpha_j)\). In the universal Sylvester matrix the top-degree coefficient row vanishes when \(a=0\), since the two leading coefficients are \(a\) and \(ea\). Its determinant polynomial in the integer coefficients is therefore divisible by the indeterminate \(a\). Specialization gives an integer discriminant. For \(e=1\) the discriminant is \(1\).

If the largest \(u_i\) comes from a nonreal root, its conjugate ties it, so \(u_2=u_1\). From \hyperref[eq:4]{(4)} one has \(c_1=c_2\le e-1\) and \(\sum_{i>1}c_i\le e-1\). A real root with positive limiting closeness actually tends to \(\pi\): if \(|\alpha|\ge2\pi+2\) then \(|\pi-\alpha|/B\ge1/2\), incompatible with a ratio tending to zero. Bounded \(B\) now gives the ordinary convergence.

\textbf{The resultant.} For this real root \(\alpha_1\), \([\mathbb Q(\alpha_1,i):\mathbb Q(\alpha_1)]=2\) because \(\mathbb Q(\alpha_1)\subset\mathbb R\). The tower formula gives \([\mathbb Q(i,\alpha_1):\mathbb Q(i)]=e\). Thus \(f\) is irreducible over \(\mathbb Q(i)\). If \(R\in\mathbb Z[i][Y]\) and \(R(\alpha_1)\ne0\), it follows that \(f\) and \(R\) are coprime and

\[
 1\le|\operatorname{Res}(f,R)|
 =|a|^{\deg R}\prod_{i=1}^e |R(\alpha_i)|
 =\mathcal M(f)^{\deg R}\prod_{i=1}^e
               \frac{|R(\alpha_i)|}{B_i^{\deg R}}.                         \tag{5}\label{eq:5}
\]

For completeness, the Sylvester matrix represents \((U,V)\mapsto Uf+VR\) with \(\deg U<\deg R\), \(\deg V<e\). Its entries are Gaussian integers. Its determinant is nonzero precisely when the two polynomials have no common factor. To obtain the product formula, split the codomain by division by \(f\). Removing the leading coefficients in the quotient block gives \(a^{\deg R}\) up to sign. On the remainder block, multiplication by \(R\) on \(\mathbb C[Y]/(f)\) is diagonal in the evaluations at the distinct \(\alpha_i\), with diagonal entries \(R(\alpha_i)\). Formula \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)} is where the bounds at different roots must refer to the same \(R\).

\textbf{Computing the determinant in two bases.} Write \(r=\deg R\). In the target space of degrees \(<e+r\), take the basis \[
 1,Y,\ldots,Y^{e-1}, f,Yf,\ldots,Y^{r-1}f .
\] The change from this basis to powers of \(Y\) is triangular with determinant \(a^r\). Polynomial division expresses the target as a remainder of degree \(<e\) and a quotient of degree \(<r\) times \(f\). Order the source as \((V,U)\). The map \(VR+Uf\) then has diagonal blocks ``multiply by \(R\) modulo \(f\)'' and the \(r\)-dimensional identity. Evaluation of a remainder at the \(e\) distinct roots is invertible by polynomial interpolation. In these coordinates the first block is diagonal with entries \(R(\alpha_i)\). Multiplication by the basis determinant proves \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)} up to sign before absolute values. This argument also works for constant nonzero \(R\) with an empty quotient block.

The field-degree calculation in the text uses \([\mathbb Q(i,\alpha_1):\mathbb Q]=2e\) and \([\mathbb Q(i):\mathbb Q]=2\). Thus the minimal polynomial of \(\alpha_1\) over \(\mathbb Q(i)\) already has degree \(e\). Any polynomial over this field vanishing at any conjugate of \(f\) is divisible by \(f\). Consequently selection at just one root ensures the product in \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)} is nonzero. A Gaussian integer \(u+iv\) has squared modulus \(u^2+v^2\), a positive integer whenever \((u,v)\ne(0,0)\). This is the discrete lower bound against which all later estimates are measured.

\section{3. An auxiliary determinant nonzero at a prescribed point}\label{an-auxiliary-determinant-nonzero-at-a-prescribed-point}

At this stage the point \(\xi\) is prescribed, and it might itself be algebraic. A polynomial determinant that is nonzero somewhere would give no information about its value there. We build a tall matrix and prove that its columns are independent at \(\xi\). The row selection must keep every polynomial order below any retained order at the same node, since the Vandermonde estimate in Section 5 uses precisely these prefixes. The index \(\sigma\) is a polynomial degree in the frequency variable and, through coefficient extraction, a derivative order along the exponential trajectory.

Fix positive rational bandwidths \(1=u_0\ge\cdots\ge u_T\) and row masses \(a_v>0\) at the integer nodes \(-V\le v\le V\), with \(L=\sum_v a_v=2\sum_j u_j\). Section 8 specifies two choices. For an integer \(N\to\infty\), set \(U_j=\lfloor u_jN\rfloor\). There is one column \((t,j)\) for each integer \(|t|\le U_j\), and \(n=\sum_{j=0}^T(2U_j+1)=LN+O(1)\) columns in total. With \(Z=iY/2\), define

\[
 A_{v,\sigma;t,j}(Y)=2^j i^{vt}(\partial_t+vZ)^j p_\sigma(t),\qquad
 p_\sigma(t)=t^\sigma/\sigma! .                                           \tag{6}\label{eq:6}
\]

Here \(\partial_t\) differentiates a polynomial before substitution of the integer \(t\). A prefix at \(v\) consists of the rows \(0,\ldots,m_v-1\). Replacing their \(p_\sigma\) by any polynomials of degree \(\sigma\) with the same leading coefficients amounts to a triangular change of determinant one. A different leading coefficient rescales a prefix determinant by the product of the leading-coefficient ratios.

\textbf{Lemma 3.1 (selection).} Group the nodes by their residue modulo four. Let \(B_J\) be the sum of the largest \(J+1\) masses in each group, taking every available mass in shorter groups. If \(B_J\ge2\sum_{j=0}^J u_j\) for \(0\le J\le T\), then for any \(\xi\ne0\) there are prefixes with \(m_v=a_v N+O(1)\) giving a square determinant \(R^0(Y)\) with \(R^0(\xi)\ne0\). The error bound is independent of \(\xi\).

For orientation, at \(j=0\) the entry is \(i^{vt}t^\sigma/\sigma!\). At \(j=1\) it is \(2i^{vt}(p_\sigma'(t)+vZp_\sigma(t))\). All layers contain the frequency \(0\), and every frequency of layer \(j\) occurs in each lower layer. This nesting enters twice, in bounding a polynomial width and in shifting a row dependence.

\emph{Proof.} Scan first through \(h_v=\lfloor a_vN\rfloor+T+2\) rows at each node. A column dependence at \(Y=\xi\) would give a nonzero Laurent polynomial \(P(y,z)=\sum_{j=0}^JP_j(y)z^j\), \(P_J\ne0\), with exponents of \(P_j\) in \([-U_j,U_j]\). With \(Z=i\xi/2\), the functions \(P(i^ve^w,w+vZ)\) would vanish to order at least \(h_v\) at \(w=0\). Indeed the \(w^\sigma\) coefficient of \((w+vZ)^je^{tw}\) is \((\partial_t+vZ)^jp_\sigma(t)\), and \(2^j\) can be absorbed into column coefficients.

In detail, \(e^{tw}=\sum_{\sigma\ge0}p_\sigma(t)w^\sigma\) and \(\partial_t e^{tw}=we^{tw}\). Acting by \((\partial_t+vZ)^j\) on this series proves the coefficient formula for every integer \(t\), positive or negative. A putative relation gives coefficients of the distinct monomials \(y^t z^j\) of \(P\), after incorporating \(2^j\). The order of a zero is the least index of a nonzero Taylor coefficient, with order \(+\infty\) for the zero germ. The \(h_v\) row equations annihilate exactly the coefficients of indices \(0,\ldots,h_v-1\) along the trajectory.

Set \(\delta=y\partial_y+\partial_z\) and form

\[
 W(y,z)=\det\big(\delta^r\partial_z^k P(y,z)\big)_{0\le r,k\le J}.
\]

The operator \(\delta\) differentiates along \(w\mapsto (y_0e^w,z_0+w)\) by the chain rule. To compare a zero count with an algebraic degree we must first eliminate the auxiliary variable \(z\) from \(W\).

\textbf{Translation and width.} Denote column \(k\) by \(C_k(z)\). Taylor expansion terminates at \(J-k\) and \(\delta\) commutes with \(\partial_z\), so \[
 C_k(z+c)=\sum_{m=0}^{J-k}C_{k+m}(z)c^m/m!.
\] Right multiplication by this triangular matrix preserves the determinant. This holds for every constant \(c\), hence evaluating at \(c=-z\) pointwise gives \(W(y,z)=W(y,0)\). In \(\partial_z^kP\) only terms from \(j\ge k\) survive. Their \(y\)-exponents belong to \([-U_k,U_k]\), and neither part of \(\delta\) changes an exponent of \(y\). Each determinant product has exponents between \(-\sum_k U_k\) and \(\sum_k U_k\). A nonzero Laurent polynomial in this range has at most \(2\sum_kU_k\) nonzero zeros with multiplicity, by multiplication by a power of \(y\).

\textbf{Why this determinant is nonzero.} On a local branch \(z=\log y\), consider the \(J+1\) functions \((\partial_z^kP)(y,\log y)\). We show that they are independent over \(\mathbb C\), then compute the leading coefficient of their Wronskian.

To check independence, suppose \(\sum_k b_k(\partial_z^kP)(y,\log y)\) vanishes identically on a branch. If \(k_0\) is the smallest index with \(b_{k_0}\ne0\), the coefficient of \((\log y)^{J-k_0}\) is \(b_{k_0}J!P_J(y)/(J-k_0)!\). Choose a base point where this Laurent polynomial is nonzero. A circuit around zero adds \(2\pi i\) to \(\log y\) and returns every Laurent coefficient to its original value. The zero germ continues to zero on each circuit. The resulting polynomial in a complex variable would vanish at all \(\log y+2m\pi i\) with a nonzero leading coefficient. This is impossible.

A Wronskian is the determinant whose columns are functions and whose rows are successive derivatives. In a finite-dimensional space of independent analytic germs choose a germ of least Taylor order, use it to eliminate that coefficient from the others, and repeat in the remaining subspace. The dimension falls by one at each step, leaving orders \(d_0<\cdots<d_J\) and leading coefficients \(b'_k\ne0\). In a local coordinate \(w\) the coefficient at order \(\sum_k d_k-J(J+1)/2\) in the derivative determinant is \((\prod b'_k)\det((d_k)_{\underline r})\), where \((d)_{\underline r}=d(d-1)\cdots(d-r+1)\). These falling powers are monic of degrees \(r\), so the determinant equals \(\prod_{i<k}(d_k-d_i)\). Here one may take \(y=y_0 e^w,z=\log y_0+w\). Differentiation in \(w\) then acts as \(\delta\). This proves the required nonvanishing of \(W\).

The four possible values \(1,i,-1,-i\) give only four places to count zeros of \(W\). Distinct nodes above one of these places yield distinct conditions, because they translate the second variable by different amounts.

\textbf{Counting together the nodes at one phase.} At the point \(i^r\), use as base functions \(F_k(w)=(\partial_z^kP)(i^re^w,w)\). Translation gives \[
  P(i^re^w,w+vZ)=\sum_{k=0}^J (vZ)^kF_k(w)/k!.
\] For \(s'\le J+1\) distinct nodes with this phase, the first \(s'\) coordinates of their coefficient vectors form a Vandermonde matrix in the numbers \(vZ\), scaled by reciprocal factorials. Its determinant is nonzero since \(Z\ne0\). Thus all \(s'\) combinations can be used as basis columns \textbf{at the same phase}. In column \(v\) the row of derivative order \(r'\le J\) has order at least \(\max(0,h_v-r')\ge(h_v-J)_+\). Every determinant product gets their sum as a lower bound. In the coordinate \(y=i^re^w\) the multiplicity at \(y=i^r\) equals that at \(w=0\) because \(dy/dw\ne0\). Take the largest \(J+1\) masses available in each class. Since \(h_v-J=\lfloor a_vN\rfloor+T+2-J>a_vN\), the sum of the four multiplicities exceeds \(NB_J\ge2N\sum_{k\le J}u_k\ge2\sum_{k\le J}U_k\). The contradiction proves full column rank before any greedy choices are made.

It remains to retain prefixes. Use monomials temporarily and scan rows in increasing order of \(\sigma-\lfloor a_vN\rfloor\), breaking ties by one fixed order of \(v\). Keep each independent row. Multiplication of a row polynomial by \(t\) acts by the same right-hand matrix at every node, since

\[
 (\partial_t+vZ)^j(tp)=t(\partial_t+vZ)^jp+j(\partial_t+vZ)^{j-1}p .
\]

Let \(r_{v,\sigma}\) be the row for the unnormalized monomial \(t^\sigma\), retaining the \(2^j\) in each column. There is one matrix \(M\) on the column space, independent of \(v\) and \(\sigma\), given for a row vector \(r\) by \[
 (rM)_{t,j}=t\,r_{t,j}+2j\,r_{t,j-1},
\] where the second summand is zero at \(j=0\). Both column phases agree and \(|t|\le U_j\) ensures that \((t,j-1)\) exists. The displayed product rule says \(r_{v,\sigma+1}=r_{v,\sigma}M\). If a scanned row is a combination of earlier rows, multiplying that equality by \(M\) expresses its successor in terms of the successors of those earlier rows. Shifting each key by \(+1\) preserves the order, including the tie rule. Thus all these successors occur before the row now in question. Every previously scanned row is in the span of the previously retained rows by induction, even when some of those successors were themselves skipped. Induction on the order at this node proves the permanent skip.

The last key for the tall matrix is \(T+1\) at every node. The sum of the candidate counts is \(LN+O(1)=n+O(1)\), while rank \(n\) is reached by that key. Among those candidates only \(O(1)\) in total can fail to be retained, counting any candidates after stopping. At each \(v\) we therefore have a prefix of length \(\lfloor a_vN\rfloor+O(1)\). Restoring factorials changes the determinant by a nonzero constant. Both the number of candidates and the rank target were independent of \(\xi\). This proves Lemma 3.1. \(\square\)

\section{4. Degree and denominators of the auxiliary polynomial}\label{degree-and-denominators-of-the-auxiliary-polynomial}

In \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)} a unit increase in \(\deg R\) costs \(\log\mathcal M(f)\), and a common denominator multiplies all the conjugate values. These costs can determine the sign of the final inequality. We estimate them by choosing different polynomial bases for the same prefixes. All determinant changes used for estimates are tracked, so the root selected in Section 3 still certifies nonvanishing.

Within each residue class order nodes by decreasing \(m_v\) and assign ranks \(q_v=0,1,\ldots\). In the limiting sums the order is decreasing \(a_v\). If two limiting masses agree, permuting their ranks simply permutes summands with that same mass, both in \hyperref[eq:7]{(7)} and in the level counts of \hyperref[eq:9]{(9)}. Put

\[
 \bar j=\frac{2\sum_j ju_j}{L},\qquad \bar q=\frac{\sum_v q_va_v}{L},
 \qquad s=\frac{\sum_v a_v^2}{2L},\qquad b_j=\frac{u_j}{2}.                \tag{7}\label{eq:7}
\]

\textbf{Lemma 4.1 (degree).} The determinant \(R^0\) is divisible by \(Y^{L_0}\) with \(L_0=\sum_vq_vm_v\). If \(\zeta\) ranges over \(\{0,u_0,\ldots,u_T\}\), set

\[
 \rho(\zeta)=-1+\frac{2\sum_j\min(\zeta,u_j)+
                   \sum_{v=-V}^{V}\sum_{k\ge0}(a_v-2\zeta-2k)_+}{L},
 \qquad D=\bar j-\bar q-\max_\zeta\rho(\zeta).                             \tag{8}\label{eq:8}
\]

Only finitely many terms in the sum over \(k\) are positive. Then \(\deg(R^0/Y^{L_0})\le n(D+o(1))\).

\emph{Proof.} At fixed \(\sigma\) and fixed residue all phases \(i^{vt}\) agree. Replace the row at \(v\) by its Newton divided difference on the first \(q_v+1\) nodes in that group. To recall, the order-\(q\) difference maps \(g\) to \(\sum_{h=0}^q g(v_h)/\prod_{r\ne h}(v_h-v_r)\), the leading coefficient of its interpolation polynomial. It kills powers of \(v\) below \(q\). In \hyperref[eq:6]{(6)} every \(v^h\) remaining after removal of the common phase carries \(Y^h\). Thus \(Y^{q_v}\) divides each transformed row. The change has nonzero constant determinant, proving the origin assertion.

For distinct nodes \(v_0,\ldots,v_q\), interpolate \(g\) by \(\sum_h g(v_h)\prod_{r\ne h}(v-v_r)/(v_h-v_r)\). Reading its coefficient of \(v^q\) gives the difference in the proof. For \(g(v)=v^h\), \(h<q\), interpolation returns the same polynomial, with that coefficient zero. The longer prefixes at all earlier nodes contain the required row \(\sigma\). The transformation is triangular, its diagonal entry at \(q\) being \(1/\prod_{r<q}(v_q-v_r)\). For example, \(v_0=0\) and \(v_1=4\) have the same phase at every integer \(t\). At \(j=1\) their entries before differencing are \(2p_\sigma'(t)\) and \(2p_\sigma'(t)+8Zp_\sigma(t)\). Their first divided difference is \(2Zp_\sigma(t)\), a multiple of \(Y\) even though either original entry can have a nonzero constant term. After extracting the common phase, an entry expands as \(2^j\sum_{h=0}^j\binom jh v^h(iY/2)^h p_\sigma^{(j-h)}(t)\). Only \(h\ge q\) remain, proving the row factor even when the transformed entry is zero. Each of the \(m_v\) rows contributes \(q_v\) to \(L_0\).

For the upper degree we are free to return to the original rows. The polynomial used in the next argument can be written explicitly as a product of powers of \(t-t_0\) at the layer-zero integer frequencies \(t_0\), completed by a power of \(t\) and divided by \(\sigma!\). It has the original leading coefficient. Determinant-one basis changes may be different in the upper-degree and lower-degree calculations.

At the other end fix \(\zeta\), put \(H_0=2U_0+1\), \(H_\zeta=2\lfloor\zeta N\rfloor+1\), and \(r_\sigma=\lfloor(\sigma-H_\zeta)/H_0\rfloor\). When \(r_\sigma\ge0\), choose a degree-\(\sigma\) row polynomial with zeros of multiplicities \(r_\sigma+\mathbf1_{|t|\le\zeta N}\) at the integer frequencies of layer zero. These multiplicities total at most \(\sigma\). Multiply by a monomial and normalize the leading term. When \(r_\sigma=-1\), impose no zeros. Every nonzero entry needs at least \(r_\sigma+\mathbf1_{|t|\le\zeta N}\) derivatives of its row polynomial. Each derivative lowers its possible \(Y\)-degree by one. Sum this loss over a determinant permutation. The average of the column indicator tends to \(2\sum_j\min(\zeta,u_j)/L\). The average of \(r_\sigma\) tends to \(-1+L^{-1}\sum_v\sum_{k\ge0}(a_v-2\zeta-2k)_+\) by counting the rows \(\sigma\ge H_\zeta+kH_0\). Subtract the origin order as well to obtain \hyperref[eq:8]{(8)}. \(\square\)

Indeed, the total prescribed multiplicity is \(r_\sigma H_0+H_\zeta\le\sigma\) if \(r_\sigma\ge0\). For \(r_\sigma=-1\) the claimed minimum loss is either \(-1\) or \(0\), valid without a zero constraint. Each selected entry can have \(Y\)-degree at most \(j-r_\sigma-\mathbf1_{\{|t|\le\zeta N\}}\). Summing over a permutation uses every row and column exactly once. To compute the row sum write \[
 r_\sigma=-1+\sum_{k\ge0}\mathbf1_{\{\sigma\ge H_\zeta+kH_0\}} .
\] In a prefix of length \(m_v\) each count is \((m_v-H_\zeta-kH_0)_+\). Dividing by \(n\) gives the positive parts in \hyperref[eq:8]{(8)}, since \(m_v/N\to a_v\), \(H_\zeta/N\to2\zeta\) and \(H_0/N\to2\). The column indicator occurs \(2\min(U_j,\lfloor\zeta N\rfloor)+1\) times in layer \(j\). The unrestricted degree sum per column tends to \(\bar j\), and \(L_0/n\to\bar q\). Every listed \(\zeta\) gives an upper bound, so using the largest proved loss yields \(D\). The finite list suffices even without optimizing over other \(\zeta\).

\textbf{Lemma 4.2 (denominators).} There is an integer \(B_N>0\) for which \(R=B_NR^0/Y^{L_0}\in\mathbb Z[i][Y]\) and \(\log B_N\le (G+o(1))nN\). The following finite formula is valid. Put \(A=\max(\max a_v,\max b_j)\) and take either \(\eta=1/30\) or \(\eta=1/24\). For \(y\ge\eta\) define

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 R_h(y)&=L^{-1}\sum_v(a_v-(h-q_v)_+y)_+,&
 C_h(y)&=4L^{-1}\sum_j(b_j-(h-j)_+y)_+,\\
 F_\eta(x)&=\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{(x-m\eta)_+^2}{2m}.&&
 \end{aligned}
\] \[
 \begin{aligned}
 G_\eta={}&\eta(\bar j-\bar q)+
 \frac{\sum_v F_\eta(a_v)-4\sum_j F_\eta(b_j)}{L}\\
 &-\sum_{h\ge1}\int_\eta^{A}(R_h(y)-C_h(y))_+\,dy,\qquad
 G=\min(G_{1/30},G_{1/24}).
 \end{aligned}\tag{9}\label{eq:9}
\]

\textbf{Arithmetic background.} For a nonzero rational number \(a/b\), \(v_p(a/b)\) is the exponent of \(p\) in \(a\) minus its exponent in \(b\). A coefficient is \(p\)-integral if this is nonnegative (zero is allowed). Apply the criterion separately to the real and imaginary rational parts. Sums and products of such pairs remain \(p\)-integral. A polynomial that takes integer values at every integer need not have integer coefficients. For example \(t(t-1)/2\) does. The \textbf{forward finite difference} is \(\Delta p(t)=p(t+1)-p(t)\). It lowers the degree of a nonconstant polynomial by one because its highest powers cancel. The polynomials \(\binom t\sigma=t(t-1)\cdots(t-\sigma+1)/\sigma!\) have the required leading terms. At a negative integer \(-m\) their values are \((-1)^\sigma\binom{m+\sigma-1}{\sigma}\). In degree at most \(S\) they form a basis. Pascal's identity gives \(\Delta\binom tr=\binom t{r-1}\). Consequently the coefficient of \(\binom tr\) in any integer-valued polynomial is the integer \(\Delta^r p(0)\), as is seen by applying \(\Delta^r\) to the basis and evaluating at \(0\).

\emph{Proof.} Work one rational prime \(p\) at a time on both rational components of Gaussian coefficients. Let \(S=\max m_v+2U_0+2\). For \(p\le\sqrt S\) use the row basis \(\binom t\sigma\). On polynomials of degree at most \(S\), differentiation equals \(\log(1+\Delta)=\sum_{k=1}^S(-1)^{k-1}\Delta^k/k\), where \(\Delta p(t)=p(t+1)-p(t)\). This follows by expanding the translation operator \(\exp(\partial_t)\) on the finite polynomial space. Differences of integer-valued polynomials stay integer-valued. Thus one derivative costs at most \(\lfloor\log_p S\rfloor\) powers of \(p\), and \(2^j\) clears the powers of two from \(Z\). The sum of logarithmic costs in this range is \(O_T(n\sqrt S\log^2 S)=o(nN)\), even bounding the number of primes by \(\sqrt S\).

On the degree-\(S\) space, \(\partial_t^{S+1}=\Delta^{S+1}=0\). Taylor's formula makes \(\exp(\partial_t)p(t)=p(t+1)\), so the formal inverse series for the logarithm and exponential become exact finite operator identities. The denominators of \(\Delta^k/k\) divide \(\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,S)\). Repeating the operator \(r\) times costs at most \(r\lfloor\log_p S\rfloor\) powers of \(p\). In an entry \(r\le j\le T\), and \(2^j(vZ)^{j-r}\) has integral Gaussian coefficients. This proves the stated small-prime bound. For larger \(p\), this estimate would miss a substantial saving. A basis adapted to spacing four reveals the degrees that each denominator consumes.

For larger \(p\) use \(4^\sigma\binom{(t+U_0)/4}{\sigma}\), with the same leading term. In each column class \((j,l)\), \(t\equiv l\bmod4\), let \(t_{jl}\) be the first frequency. Take successive forward differences in \((t-t_{jl})/4\). This triangular column operation has determinant one. Its \(k\)th difference inserts \(x^k\) in the generating series \((1+x)^{(t+U_0)/4}\). Up to \(p\)-units, the entries are

\[
 [x^{\sigma-k}](1+x)^{(t_{jl}+U_0)/4}
        \big(vZ+\tfrac14\log(1+x)\big)^j .                               \tag{10}\label{eq:10}
\]

Write \(\lambda=(t_{jl}+U_0)/4\). Differentiating \((1+x)^{(t+U_0)/4}\) with respect to \(t\) multiplies it by \(\log(1+x)/4\). Forward difference in the integer frequency index increases the exponent by one, so subtracting the previous power multiplies it by \(x\). If \(C_a\) is original column \(a\) in the class, difference column \(k\) is \(\sum_{a=0}^k(-1)^{k-a}\binom ka C_a\). This is triangular with diagonal one. The phase stays constant within the class, and the omitted \(4^\sigma\), \(2^j\) and phase are units at odd primes. For the row divided difference the phase is common to nodes of a fixed residue.

For odd \(p\), every coefficient of the rational power is \(p\)-integral, meaning its denominator in lowest terms is prime to \(p\). Indeed, for the coefficient \(\binom{\lambda}{r}\) with \(4\lambda\) integral, take an integer \(m\ge r\) with \(4m\equiv4\lambda\pmod {p^{v_p(r!)+1}}\), possible since \(p\) is odd. The two falling products of \(r\) factors agree to that precision. The one at \(m\) is \(r!\binom mr\), so division by \(r!\) introduces no \(p\) in the reduced denominator of the one at \(\lambda\). The row divided difference kills powers of \(v\) below \(q_v\), leaving at most \(j-q_v\) logarithm factors. Node differences are units for these \(p\) once \(N\) is large. A logarithm coefficient of degree \(\le S\) has at most one \(p\) in its denominator, and such a term costs at least \(p\) degrees in \(x\). A nonzero entry requires \(\sigma\ge k\), \(j\ge q_v\). Its denominator exponent is therefore at most

\[
 \min\big(j-q_v,\ \lfloor\sigma/p\rfloor-\lfloor k/p\rfloor\big).
\]

To see the precision in the binomial argument, \(m-\lambda\) is a multiple of \(p^{v_p(r!)+1}\) in rationals of denominator prime to \(p\). Hence the products \((m)_{\underline r}\) and \((\lambda)_{\underline r}\) differ by a multiple of the same power. The first product is divisible by \(p^{v_p(r!)}\), and the second inherits this divisibility. For instance \(\binom{1/4}{3}=7/128\), where the factor of \(3\) from \(3!\) cancels, while \([x^3]\log(1+x)=1/3\) really does cost a factor of \(3\). This small example illustrates which type of coefficient causes a denominator. The large-prime counting argument uses \(p>\sqrt S\). The logarithm has coefficients \(\pm1/a\) in degree \(a\) and can still have denominators. Since \(a\le S<p^2\), a single coefficient costs at most one power of \(p\) and then \(a\ge p\). Thus a product costing \(d'\) powers needs \(d'p\le\sigma-k\). We have \(\lfloor(\sigma-k)/p\rfloor\le\lfloor\sigma/p\rfloor-\lfloor k/p\rfloor\). The number of logarithms is also at most \(j-q_v\) because a surviving \(v\)-degree is at least \(q_v\). These two bounds refer to the same matrix entry.

The first bound summed over a permutation is \(\sum j-\sum q_v\) (with row and column multiplicities). The second sum can be diminished by at least

\[
 E_p=\sum_{h\ge1}
 \big(\#\{\text{rows}:\lfloor\sigma/p\rfloor+q_v\ge h\}
       -\#\{\text{columns}: j+\lfloor k/p\rfloor\ge h\}\big)_+ .          \tag{11}\label{eq:11}
\]

Indeed, for each matched row and column the saving is the positive part of the difference of the two integer levels in \hyperref[eq:11]{(11)}. At height \(h\), each surplus row above that level must match a column below it. Sum over \(h\). All coefficients obey the same bounds. Restoring the original determinant multiplies by fixed node differences, which introduce no new large-prime denominators.

\textbf{A matching calculation.} Write \(A=\lfloor\sigma/p\rfloor\), \(K=\lfloor k/p\rfloor\). Relative to the floor cost \(A-K\), the saving on one edge of a nonzero permutation is \[
 (A-K)-\min(A-K,j-q_v)=((A+q_v)-(K+j))_+ .
\] For two nonnegative levels \(r,s\), \((r-s)_+\) counts integer thresholds \(h\) for which \(r\ge h>s\). If the row levels are \(3,1\) and the column levels \(2,0\), threshold \(3\) forces one crossing, threshold \(2\) forces none, and threshold \(1\) forces one. Pairing \(3\) with \(2\) and \(1\) with \(0\) saves \(2\). Reversing that pairing would save \(3\). The threshold count is a guaranteed saving independent of which available matching occurs. It can therefore be subtracted from the floor cost for the determinant as a whole. It is an arithmetic matching of levels. The labels in Section 6 are chosen by a binomial expansion and will require a different count.

\textbf{From prime sums to the integral.} We use the prime number theorem in the form \(\vartheta(t)=t+o(t)\) for \(\vartheta(t)=\sum_{p\le t}\log p\). Appendix C.2 proves this version. For fixed \(0\le l<r\) it gives \[
 N^{-1}\sum_{lN<p\le rN}\log p=(\vartheta(rN)-\vartheta(lN))/N\longrightarrow r-l .
\] A bounded continuous piecewise affine function on \([\eta,A]\) can be squeezed between its minimum and maximum on each cell of a finite partition. Its weighted prime sums lie between the corresponding lower and upper sums times the interval masses just computed. Take \(N\to\infty\) first and then let the largest cell shrink: uniform continuity makes the difference tend to zero. A uniformly \(O(1/N)\) error in the integrand has total cost \(O(\vartheta(AN+O(1))/N^2)=o(1)\). The bounded number of primes possibly crossing the rounded upper endpoint costs \(O(\log N/N)\).

Use the first bound up to \(p\le\eta N\). The prime number theorem in the form \(\vartheta(x)=\sum_{p\le x}\log p=x+o(x)\) {[}3{]} gives the first term in \hyperref[eq:9]{(9)}. For \(p/N=y\ge\eta\), the normalized counts in \hyperref[eq:11]{(11)} are \(R_h(y)+O(1/N)\) and \(C_h(y)+O(1/N)\) because one column class has \(b_jN+O(1)\) consecutive differences, and there are four classes per layer. Only boundedly many \(h\) occur. For example the normalized sum of \(\lfloor\sigma/p\rfloor\) for a row prefix of length \(xN+O(1)\) is \(L^{-1}\sum_{m\ge1}(x-my)_++O(1/N)\). Its integral from \(\eta\) to \(A\) is \(F_\eta(x)/L\). The same count for the four column classes is subtracted. Prime sums weighted by \(\log p/N\) converge to these integrals by the prime number theorem on fixed intervals followed by approximation by step functions. Beyond \(AN+O(1)\) the floor cost is zero. Multiplying the primes to their verified nonnegative integer powers clears every coefficient. This proves \hyperref[eq:9]{(9)}. \(\square\)

More explicitly, the sum of \(\lfloor\sigma/p\rfloor\) over one prefix is \(\sum_{m\ge1}(m_v-mp)_+\). A threshold \(h\) in \hyperref[eq:11]{(11)} instead requires \(\sigma\ge(h-q_v)_+p\), giving \(R_h\). A column class has length \(b_jN+O(1)\) because a quarter of \(2u_jN+O(1)\) consecutive frequencies lie in it. Its analogous threshold is \((h-j)_+p\). Four classes give \(C_h\). For \(p\ge\eta N\) the number of nonempty summands is bounded independently of \(N\). Since \(A\ge x\) for each mass or class length \(x\), integration of \((x-my)_+\) gives the triangle area \((x-m\eta)_+^2/(2m)\). For \(\sqrt S<p\le\eta N\) the first bound per permutation divided by \(n\) tends to \(\bar j-\bar q\), and the mass of primes in that range divided by \(N\) tends to \(\eta\). The small-prime cost divided by \(nN\) tends to zero as \(S=O(N)\). For each cutoff this yields a denominator multiplier. Taking the better asymptotic upper bound proves \hyperref[eq:9]{(9)}. One can always take the least common denominator of the actual coefficients. Multiplying a nonzero value by \(B_N/\xi^{L_0}\) preserves nonvanishing at \(\xi\ne0\).

\section{5. The size of a determinant along the boundary}\label{the-size-of-a-determinant-along-the-boundary}

Bounding a determinant by the product of the largest entries would discard the effect of having many frequencies close together. A block of rows at one node gives a product of pairwise frequency differences. Its logarithm becomes an integral over pairs of points of \([-1,1]\). We derive that integral here, including what happens when two frequencies coincide, and turn a maximization over measures into an explicit upper bound.

A finite measure records how much mass lies in a set. A point mass \(\delta_x\) assigns weight one to the point \(x\) and integrates a test function \(g\) to \(g(x)\). A signed measure in this proof has a real density that may take either sign. A measure with density \(r\) integrates \(g\) to \(\int gr\,dx\). Thus a column assigned to node \(v\) places mass \(1/N\) at \(t/N\) in its block. The measure of all columns converges on each continuous test function by the Riemann-sum rule on each band.

Divide each frequency by \(N\). The limiting distribution of all columns, each with mass \(1/N\), is \(\nu(dx)=\sum_j\mathbf1_{[-u_j,u_j]}(x)\,dx\). Its total mass is \(L\). Call \((\mu_v)\) admissible if \(\mu_v\ge0\), \(\sum_v\mu_v=\nu\) and \(\int d\mu_v=a_v\). Each has a density bounded by \(T+1\). Put \(m_v(\mu)=\int x\,d\mu_v(x)\) in this section only. We use \(m_v\) without an argument for an integer number of rows. Define

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 I(\mu,\rho)&=\iint\log(2|x-y|)\,d\mu(x)d\rho(y),\\
 \mathcal F(\mathbf k)&=\frac1L\sup_\mu\sum_v
       \left(k_v m_v(\mu)+\tfrac12 I(\mu_v,\mu_v)\right),\\
 A_0&=\frac1{2L}\sum_v a_v^2\big(\tfrac32-\log(2a_v)\big).
 \end{aligned}                                                            \tag{12}\label{eq:12}
\]

Here \(\sup_\mu\) ranges over admissible distributions on \([-1,1]\). The kernel is integrable across the diagonal for bounded densities. Subscripts on \(f_0\) denote the zero field, independently of the minimal polynomial \(f\). Let \(f_0\) be a certified upper bound for \(\mathcal F(\mathbf0)\). For the integral polynomial of Lemma 4.2,

\[
 \log\mathcal L(R)\ \le\ nN(A_0+G+f_0+o(1)).                              \tag{13}\label{eq:13}
\]

The kernel is the function \(K(x,y)=\log(2|x-y|)\). Its negative singularity is integrable against bounded densities: for each \(x\), the absolute integral on \(|x-y|\le\epsilon<1/2\) is bounded by \(2\epsilon(1-\log(2\epsilon))\) times a density bound. The integral \(I\) is a bilinear form on real bounded densities, including signed ones. The field term is linear in the assigned density. The constraints allow splitting a frequency between nodes in the limit. For instance \(\mu_v=(a_v/L)\nu\) is admissible.

To prove this and its boundary variant, expand the determinant by row blocks indexed by \(v\). For the \(m=m_v\) columns assigned to one block and any orders \(0\le j'_c\le T\), remove column phases and constants. For bounded \(z_v\) the remaining block equals

\[
 \det\big((\partial_{t_c}+z_v)^{j'_c}p_\sigma(t_c)\big)_{\sigma,c}
 =\frac{\prod_c(\partial_{t_c}+z_v)^{j'_c}\,
               \prod_{c<c'}(t_{c'}-t_c)}{\prod_{\sigma=0}^{m-1}\sigma!}.   \tag{14}\label{eq:14}
\]

We prove \hyperref[eq:14]{(14)}, estimate the differentiated product, and keep track of the factorial denominator. An integral estimate for factorials gives the terms of Stirling's formula {[}4{]} that survive at the scale \(nN\).

\textbf{The block computation.} In the determinant \(\det(t_c^\sigma)\) interchanging two columns reverses the sign, so each \(t_{c'}-t_c\) divides it. Both the product of differences and the determinant have degree \(\binom m2\), and the coefficient of \(t_1^0t_2^1\cdots t_m^{m-1}\) is \(1\) in both. Division of each row by \(\sigma!\) gives the denominator of \hyperref[eq:14]{(14)}. In each determinant permutation exactly one factor depends on \(t_c\). Applying its differential operator affects just that factor. Thus the equality continues to hold after differentiation when evaluation points coincide. A useful test is \(m=2\) with \(j'_1=0,j'_2=1,z_v=0\): differentiating \(t_2-t_1\) gives \(1\) even at \(t_1=t_2\). Setting the variables equal before differentiating would lose that term.

On the product of circles \(|\tau_c-t_c|=1\) one has \(|\tau_{c'}-\tau_c|\le |t_{c'}-t_c|+2\). Iterated Cauchy integrals for orders \(r_c\) cost at most \(\prod_c r_c!\) times this supremum. All factors of the chosen radius are one. Expanding \((\partial+z_v)^{j'_c}\) gives at most \(\sum_{r=0}^{j'_c}\binom{j'_c}r r! |z_v|^{j'_c-r}\) per column. With \(T\) fixed and the \(z_v\) in a fixed bounded set, the logarithm of this cost is \(O(m)\). Distinct blocks can have different \(z_v\). Summing over assignments of columns to nodes costs at most \(n\log(2V+1)\) in the logarithm. A zero block can be discarded.

\textbf{The cancellation of \(\log N\).} Integral comparison of the increasing function \(\log x\) on each unit interval gives \(\log(k!)=k\log k-k+O(\log(2k))\). Summing the increasing function \(x\log x\) from \(1\) to \(m\) by integrals gives \(\frac12m^2\log m-\frac14m^2+O(m\log(2m))\). Subtracting \(\sum_{k<m}k=\frac12m^2+O(m)\) gives the factorial sum in the text. This derives the amount of Stirling's formula needed here. Write \(x_c=t_c/N\). From \(\prod_{c<c'}(|t_{c'}-t_c|+2)\) pull out \(N^{m(m-1)/2}\). The explicit part of its log after subtraction of the factorial sum is \[
 \tfrac12m^2(\tfrac32-\log(m/N))+O(m\log(2N)).
\] Here \(m=a_vN+O(1)\) and \(n=LN+O(1)\). Dividing by \(nN\) and summing gives \(\sum_v a_v^2(3/2-\log a_v)/(2L)\). The pair terms still use \(\log |x-y|\). Replacing this by \(\log(2|x-y|)\) adds \(\log 2\) for each pair. Subtracting \(\sum_v a_v^2\log2/(2L)\) from the explicit part gives exactly \(A_0\).

For passage to the limit set \(\mu_{v,N}=N^{-1}\sum_c\delta_{t_c/N}\) for the columns of block \(v\). A pair sum for this discrete measure omits the diagonal. Its kernel has been smoothed at a scale \(2/N\) by the Cauchy estimate. We next justify taking an upper limit despite the logarithm's singularity.

\textbf{Why a limiting assignment gives a uniform upper bound.} Weak convergence means convergence of integrals of every continuous test function. For any sequence of assignments, use a diagonal subsequence for the cumulative block masses on rational endpoints. On an interval of length \(\delta\) the total column mass is at most \((T+1)(\delta+2/N)\), so these limiting cumulative functions extend to increasing Lipschitz functions. Their measures have densities bounded by \(T+1\), mass \(a_v\), and sum \(\nu\). Approximation of a continuous function by step values proves the asserted weak convergence. For a continuous function of two variables, partition the square into small rectangles. The product masses of rectangles converge because interval masses do, and the oscillation error tends uniformly to zero. This proves convergence for the product measures too.

Put \(K_\epsilon(x,y)=\log\max(|x-y|,\epsilon)\). For \(u=|x-y|\), \(u+2/N\le\max(u,\epsilon)(1+2/(N\epsilon))\). In the normalized unordered pair sum one half of the product integral counts also the \(m\) diagonal terms. Subtracting them costs \(O(m|\log\epsilon|/(nN))\) for fixed \(\epsilon\). This is valid even when different columns have the same frequency. The added \(\log(1+2/(N\epsilon))\) tends to zero after normalization since \(m^2/(nN)\) stays bounded. Only after \(N\to\infty\) do we send \(\epsilon\) to zero, using the uniform diagonal integral estimate above. Taking an assignment arbitrarily close to the largest bound at each \(N\) proves the upper estimate for the maximum. A sequence of boundary points on the unit circle has a convergent subsequence, so the same proof applies uniformly when \(z_v\) depends continuously on that boundary point. Finally an exponential contributes \(\sum_c t_c\operatorname{Re}z_v/(nN)\to L^{-1}\operatorname{Re}z_v\,m_v(\mu)\) blockwise. Without it we obtain \hyperref[eq:13]{(13)}: a coefficient of \(R\) is bounded by \(\max_{|Y|=1}|R(Y)|\) by Cauchy's formula, and \(\log(\deg R+1)/(nN)\to0\).

\textbf{A computable upper bound.} For signed measures with bounded real densities write \(U_\rho(x)=\int\log(2|x-y|)\,d\rho(y)\). If \(\rho_v\) are any such references and \(\beta_v\) any real numbers, then

\[
 L\mathcal F(\mathbf k)\le
 -\tfrac12\sum_v I(\rho_v,\rho_v)+\sum_v\beta_va_v+
 \int\max_v\big(k_vx+U_{\rho_v}(x)-\beta_v\big)\,d\nu(x).                \tag{15}\label{eq:15}
\]

Indeed \(I(\lambda,\lambda)\le0\) for every bounded signed density \(\lambda\). To see this, write \(x=\cos\theta\), \(y=\cos\phi\) and use the \textbf{Chebyshev polynomials of the first kind}, \(T_j(\cos\theta)=\cos(j\theta)\) {[}5{]}. Expand \(\log|1-re^{it}|=-\sum_{j\ge1}r^j\cos(jt)/j\) for \(r<1\) at \(t=\theta+\phi,\theta-\phi\), then let \(r\uparrow1\). The two logarithms add to \(\log(2|x-y|)\) in the limit. For \(r\ge1/2\), \(|1-re^{it}|^2=(1-r)^2+4r\sin^2(t/2)\) bounds each negative part by a constant plus \(|\log|\sin(t/2)||\), integrable on the angle square. Therefore

\[
 I(\lambda,\lambda)=-2\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}
            \frac{\big(\int T_j\,d\lambda\big)^2}{j}\le0 .
\]

For the energy inequality recall \(T_0=1,T_1=x,T_{j+1}=2xT_j-T_{j-1}\), so the cosine addition formula gives \(T_j(\cos\theta)=\cos(j\theta)\). Under \(x=\cos\theta\), \(0\le\theta\le\pi\), a bounded signed density changes to a bounded signed density times \(\sin\theta\). For \(r<1\) the logarithm series is absolutely summable. Adding the two cosine series uses \[
 \cos j(\theta+\phi)+\cos j(\theta-\phi)=2\cos(j\theta)\cos(j\phi).
\] At \(r=1\) the product of the two absolute values is \(4|\sin((\theta+\phi)/2)\sin((\theta-\phi)/2)|=2|\cos\theta-\cos\phi|\). The integrable logarithmic bound on the angle square follows by integrating in \(\theta\) first over an interval of length \(\pi\) and bounding by the integral of a full period. Dominated convergence applies to the integrals even with signed densities. On the series side the nonnegative quantities \(r^j(\int T_j\,d\lambda)^2/j\) increase termwise to their limits. Their sum has the stated finite integral as limit after multiplication by \(-2\). For a signed density of either sign the expression is a sum of negative squares. In particular the total mass of \(\lambda\) can be arbitrary for this normalization of the kernel.

Apply this to \(\lambda=\mu_v-\rho_v\), expand the square, and add the mass identities \(\beta_v\int d\mu_v=\beta_va_v\). The sum of the remaining integrals against \(\mu_v\) is bounded by the integral of their pointwise maximum against \(\nu\). This proves \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)} for every admissible \(\mu\), hence for the supremum. Appendix A explains how the right side is enclosed on entire intervals.

In algebraic terms, \[
 \tfrac12 I(\mu_v,\mu_v)\le I(\mu_v,\rho_v)-\tfrac12 I(\rho_v,\rho_v).
\] After adding the field, the mixed term becomes \(\int(k_vx+U_{\rho_v}(x)-\beta_v)\,d\mu_v+\beta_va_v\). Replacing each integrand by the largest over nodes is legitimate because \(d\mu_v\) is nonnegative. The sum then integrates against \(\sum_v\mu_v=\nu\). This is why arbitrary signed reference densities and arbitrary charges give valid certificates. Finding good references is an optimization problem. Checking any specified references needs only the one-sided inequality \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)}.

\section{6. Vanishing under perturbation and independent analytic paths}\label{vanishing-under-perturbation-and-independent-analytic-paths}

The length estimate treats all evaluation points alike. At a point close to \(\pi\) we can exploit the exponential that generated the phases \(i^{vt}\). The perturbation produces small factors from some columns and forces high-order zeros among others. To account for both effects we continue whole determinants of fixed column choices and use an interior bound in the complex disk.

\subsection{6.1. Expansion at a nearby point}\label{expansion-at-a-nearby-point}

Let \(\theta=i\pi/2\), \(\varepsilon=i(\alpha-\pi)/2\). The arithmetic progression of phases \(e^{t\theta v}=i^{vt}\) is the reason for the choice of \(\pi/2\). For a column of order \(j\) its value at \(\alpha\) has the exact expansion, with the harmless factor \(2^j\) omitted,

\[
 i^{vt}(\partial_t+iv\alpha/2)^jp_\sigma(t)
 =\sum_{\ell=0}^j\binom j\ell\varepsilon^\ell v^\ell
      [p_\sigma(\partial_x)(x^{j-\ell}e^{tx})]_{x=\theta v}.              \tag{16}\label{eq:16}
\]

Here \(p(\partial_x)=\sum a_r\partial_x^r\) for \(p(t)=\sum a_rt^r\). Differentiating \(e^{tx}\) in \(t\) gives \(x^me^{tx}=\partial_t^m e^{tx}\). Commuting independent differentiations and using \(p(\partial_x)e^{tx}=p(t)e^{tx}\) gives \[
 p(\partial_x)(x^me^{tx})=\partial_t^m(p(t)e^{tx})
 =e^{tx}(\partial_t+x)^mp(t).
\] Write \(iv\alpha/2=\theta v+\varepsilon v\), expand the \(j\) commuting summands by the binomial theorem, and use the identity at \(x=\theta v\). The exponential in this identity supplies exactly \(i^{vt}\).

Expand the shifted power binomially and use \(p(\partial_x)(x^m e^{tx})=e^{tx}(\partial_t+x)^mp(t)\) to verify \hyperref[eq:16]{(16)}. Fix a choice \(\ell_c\) in every column and let \(n_\ell\) count the selections equal to \(\ell\). Choose a real \(z\in(0,1)\) and real-coefficient functions \(\psi_v\) analytic near the closed unit disk with \(\psi_v(0)=0\), \(\psi_v(z)=v\). In \hyperref[eq:16]{(16)} substitute \(x=\theta\psi_v(w)\) \emph{after} differentiation, retaining \(v^{\ell_c}\). This gives an analytic determinant \(H(w)\) which reproduces exactly the chosen term at \(w=z\), after the external binomials and \(\varepsilon\) powers are restored.

\textbf{Lemma 6.1.} Its zero at \(w=0\) has order at least

\[
 h=\sum_{\ell=0}^T\binom{n_\ell}{2}-\sum_v\binom{m_v}{2}.                 \tag{17}\label{eq:17}
\]

Expand \(x^{j-\ell}e^{tx}\) into powers \(x^k\). For fixed \(\ell,k\) the vector down the rows is \(v^\ell[p_\sigma(\partial_x)x^k]_{x=\theta\psi_v(w)}\), independent of the column apart from a scalar. Repeated \(k\) within a label cannot contribute to a determinant. Distinct nonnegative integers for \(n_\ell\) columns sum to at least \(\binom{n_\ell}{2}\). A row derivative lowers the order by at most \(\sigma\), and \(\sum_{\rm rows}\sigma=\sum_v\binom{m_v}{2}\). These statements hold coefficient by coefficient in the Taylor series, since \(k\) larger than the required order plus \(\max\sigma\) does not contribute. This proves \hyperref[eq:17]{(17)}, also when its right side is negative.

\textbf{What a label controls.} For a fixed term, column \(c\) with label \(\ell\) is a sum over \(k\ge j_c-\ell\) with scalar \(t_c^{\,k-j_c+\ell}/(k-j_c+\ell)!\) multiplying the column vector specified in the proof. If two columns have the identical pair \((\ell,k)\) these vectors are proportional, giving determinant zero for every \(w\). For example, take labels \((0,0,1)\): powers \((0,0,2)\) force a zero determinant, whereas \((0,1,0)\) meet the distinctness condition. The two occurrences of power zero in the latter belong to different labels and carry \(1\) and \(v\) down the rows. The factor \(v^0\) is \(1\) also at the zero node. They need not be proportional. The smallest possible total power is thus \(1=\binom22+\binom12\) in this example. Other restrictions on \(k\) can only increase the order. Matching rows to columns for a denominator bound in \hyperref[eq:11]{(11)} gives no right to impose additional distinct powers across these labels.

At row \((v,\sigma)\) the monomial derivative is zero if \(k<\sigma\), otherwise a constant times \(\psi_v(w)^{k-\sigma}\). Since \(\psi_v(0)=0\), its order is at least \(k-\sigma\). Every permutation uses every row once. Subtracting \(\sum_{\rm rows}\sigma\) from the surviving total power gives \hyperref[eq:17]{(17)}. We keep \(v^\ell\) at its integer node value throughout. We fix \(\ell_c\) in a column before continuing so that the small \(\varepsilon\) powers are exterior scalars and the common column vectors for equal labels and powers remain available. Agreement with the arithmetic expression is required at \(w=z\). No identity between distinct continuations at other \(w\) is assumed.

On the boundary expand the \emph{same entire determinant} by node, with all rows of a prefix present. Pull the exponential and \(v^{\ell_c}\) factors from each assigned column of each block. The remaining determinant is \hyperref[eq:14]{(14)} with \(j'_c=j_c-\ell_c\). For fixed paths the auxiliary factors and all choices of labels cost \(O_{T,V,\psi}(n\log n)\) in the logarithm. Section 5 gives the boundary upper rate \(A_0+\mathcal F((\pi/2)\operatorname{Im}\boldsymbol\psi)\). The sign of the field can be reversed by reflecting all frequency measures. The error is uniform in the boundary point and in all assignments. A failure of uniformity would allow one to take a violating sequence with a convergent boundary point and weak limits, contradicting the truncated-kernel bound there.

\textbf{The Poisson--Jensen inequality on the disk.} For \(z\) real in \((0,1)\), the Poisson kernel \(P_z(\varphi)=(1-z^2)/|e^{i\varphi}-z|^2\) has mean one and Fourier coefficients \(z^{|k|}\), by multiplying the geometric series or taking the real part of \((1+ze^{i\varphi})/(1-ze^{i\varphi})\). Its average of a continuous boundary function is its harmonic extension evaluated at \(z\). This follows first for Fourier polynomials by the harmonic extensions \(r^k\cos k\varphi,r^k\sin k\varphi\), then by uniform approximation (the Fejér kernels below also approximate continuous functions uniformly). A subharmonic function satisfies the local mean-value upper inequality and is bounded above on a disk by the harmonic function with the same continuous boundary values, by the maximum principle. For an analytic \(G\) the smooth regularization \(\frac12\log(|G|^2+\epsilon)\) has Laplacian \(2\epsilon |G'|^2/(|G|^2+\epsilon)^2\ge0\), hence is subharmonic. Letting \(\epsilon\downarrow0\) gives the upper Poisson bound for \(\log|G|\). The log singularities at the finitely many boundary zeros of a nonzero function analytic past the circle are integrable, by factoring each zero. For \(H(w)=w^{h_0}G(w)\), the boundary moduli agree while \(\log|H(z)|=h_0\log z+\log|G(z)|\). A lower bound \(h\le h_0\) is enough, because \(\log z<0\). This also explains how to read a negative lower bound in \hyperref[eq:17]{(17)}.

Write \(\mathbb E_z\) for integration against the probability density \(P_z(\varphi)/(2\pi)\). Applying the Poisson--Jensen inequality just proved gives \(\log|H(z)|\le h\log z+\mathbb E_z\log|H(e^{i\varphi})|\). An identically zero determinant satisfies the upper bound with logarithm \(-\infty\). Define \(\mathcal B_z\) as the infimum of \(\mathbb E_z\mathcal F((\pi/2)\operatorname{Im}\boldsymbol\psi)\) over the admissible paths above.

If \(N=\lfloor bX\rfloor\), \(b>0\), and \(|\alpha-\pi|\le \exp(-(c-o(1))X)\) for \(c>0\), put \(\kappa=\log(1/z)\) and \(B=b\kappa L/2\). Using \hyperref[eq:16]{(16)}--\hyperref[eq:17]{(17)} and \(R=B_NR^0/Y^{L_0}\) gives

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \limsup\frac{\log|R(\alpha)|}{nX}
 &\le b(A_0+G+\kappa s+\mathcal B_z)-F_B(c),\\
 F_B(c)&=\min_{\substack{p_0,\ldots,p_T\ge0\\ \sum p_\ell=1}}
        \left(c\sum_\ell \ell p_\ell+B\sum_\ell p_\ell^2\right).
 \end{aligned}                                                            \tag{18}\label{eq:18}
\]

Indeed \(p_\ell=n_\ell/n\), \(\sum_\ell\binom{n_\ell}{2}/(nX)=(bL/2)\sum p_\ell^2+o(1)\), and \(\sum_v\binom{m_v}{2}/(nX)=bs+o(1)\). The perturbation contributes \(-c\sum\ell p_\ell\), and enlarging the feasible proportions to the full simplex provides an upper bound for all terms. Since \(\alpha\to\pi\), division by \(\alpha^{L_0}\) can only improve the bound eventually. Fixing the paths before taking \(X\to\infty\) justifies the later infimum. The derivation works at any nearby complex conjugate, even if another root was used to select \(R\).

The scale factors in \hyperref[eq:18]{(18)} can be checked before optimizing. We have \(n/X\to bL\) and \(\sum_v m_v^2/(2nX)\to b\sum_v a_v^2/(2L)=bs\). Taking logs of the perturbation multiplier gives \(\sum_\ell\ell n_\ell\log|\varepsilon|/(nX)\le-c\sum_\ell\ell p_\ell+o(1)\) uniformly since \(\ell\le T\). The zero contributes \(-\kappa bL\sum_\ell p_\ell^2/2+\kappa bs\). Combining these negative terms requires the \textbf{minimum} of their positive cost on the simplex. Any restrictions imposed by the individual layers could only increase that minimum. The number of label choices is at most \((T+1)^n\), and the binomial and fixed-node factors are exponentially bounded in \(n\). All have logarithm \(o(nX)\). For every fixed family of analytic paths we obtain the inequality before taking its infimum. Thus there is no need to choose a sequence of paths depending on \(X\).

\subsection{6.2. Bounding the mean over paths}\label{bounding-the-mean-over-paths}

We must arrange the boundary values cheaply while meeting the interior constraints \(\psi_v(z)=v\). The energy supremum is convex in the field, and adding a correction to a field has a cost bounded by a linear allocation problem. The following construction solves that allocation exactly when certain adjacent differences are nonnegative.

Write \(H_*(\mathbf d)=L^{-1}\max_\mu\sum_v d_v m_v(\mu)\). Reflection of \(x\) makes both \(H_*\) and \(\mathcal F\) even. Directly from \hyperref[eq:12]{(12)}, \(\mathcal F\) is convex and \(\mathcal F(\mathbf k+\mathbf d)\le\mathcal F(\mathbf k)+H_*(\mathbf d)\). Both functions are Lipschitz since all masses and \(|x|\) are bounded. If \(d_v\) increases with \(v\), divide \(\nu\) from left to right into consecutive intervals of masses \(a_{-V},\ldots,a_V\) and call the resulting moments \(m_v^*\). Then \(H_*(\mathbf d)=L^{-1}\sum_v d_vm_v^*\). To prove this, decompose the sum by the nonnegative increments \(d_v-d_{v-1}\). For each upper tail of nodes its moment is at most the moment of the rightmost interval having its mass, and the ordered division attains every such bound. Set \(Q=L^{-1}\sum_v v m_v^*\). For the symmetric masses used in Section 8 this gives \(m_{-v}^*=-m_v^*\).

Indeed \(|\sum_v (k_v-k'_v)m_v(\mu)|/L\le\|\mathbf k-\mathbf k'\|_\infty\). This bound passes to either supremum. For increasing \(d_v\) write \(d_v=d_{-V}+\sum_{j=-V+1}^v(d_j-d_{j-1})\). The constant term integrates against the whole measure. At level \(j\) the tail measure \(\omega=\sum_{v\ge j}\mu_v\) satisfies \(0\le\omega\le\nu\) and has specified mass. Let \(\omega^*\) be \(\nu\) on the rightmost interval \([u,1]\) of that mass. Then \(\int x\,d(\omega-\omega^*)=\int(x-u)\,d(\omega-\omega^*)\le0\): to the left of \(u\) the integrand multiplier is negative, to the right the measure difference is nonpositive. All tails are maximized at once by nested right intervals. In the two witnesses the masses are symmetric in \(v\) and \(\nu\) is even, giving the moment symmetry in the text.

Take a bounded vector step function \(\mathbf k\) on the first quadrant of the circle. Reflect it evenly across the imaginary axis, then oddly across the real axis to form \(\widetilde{\mathbf k}\). In formulas involving \(S=|\sin\varphi|\) write \(\mathbf k\) for the original values folded back into the first quadrant and use the folded probability. Set \(A_z=\pi(z^{-1}-z)/4\). If \(d_v=A_z v-\mathbb E_z[Sk_v]\) is increasing with \(v\), then

\[
 \mathcal B_z\le Q A_z-
 \mathbb E_z\left[S\sum_v k_v m_v^*/L-\mathcal F(\mathbf k)\right].       \tag{19}\label{eq:19}
\]

Here the freedom to specify different bounded functions at different nodes is compatible with the exact equalities \(\psi_v(z)=v\). For a real-coefficient analytic polynomial \(g\) with \(g(0)=0\),

\[
 \mathbb E_z[(\pi/2)\operatorname{Im}g(e^{i\varphi})\sin\varphi]=A_zg(z).
                                                                            \tag{20}\label{eq:20}
\]

For \(k\ge1\) the Poisson average of \(\cos(m\varphi)\) is \(z^{|m|}\). The identity \(2\sin(k\varphi)\sin\varphi=\cos((k-1)\varphi)-\cos((k+1)\varphi)\) therefore gives \((\pi/4)(z^{k-1}-z^{k+1})=A_z z^k\) after multiplication by \(\pi/2\). Real coefficients permit taking the imaginary part term by term. The constant term must be zero. For any path with \(g(z)=1\) this equality forces \(\mathbb E_z[(\pi/2)|\operatorname{Im}g|]\ge A_z\) since \(|\sin\varphi|\le1\).

\textbf{Constructing the prescribed boundary functions.} Extend a first-quadrant step function by the two symmetries in the text. Its oddness in \(\varphi\) ensures that its real Fourier series contains only sines. For \(m\ge1\) the Fejér kernel \[
 K_m(\tau)=m^{-1}|1+e^{i\tau}+\cdots+e^{i(m-1)\tau}|^2
\] is nonnegative, has circle mean one by orthogonality, and is a finite Fourier polynomial. For \(\delta\le|\tau|\le\pi\) it is at most \(1/(m\sin^2(\delta/2))\) by the geometric sum. Convolution with \(K_m\) preserves oddness and the sup norm bound, and at a point inside a constant interval the error tends to zero: choose \(\delta\) smaller than its distance to the interval ends and use the tail bound. Integrating the errors gives \(L^1\) convergence by dominated convergence. The identical kernel argument with uniform continuity gives uniform approximation for continuous functions. Multiplying the \(L^1\) error by the fixed bound \((1+z)/(1-z)\) for the Poisson kernel controls its weighted mean. A finite sine sum \(\sum_r a_r\sin r\varphi\) is the boundary imaginary part of \(g(w)=\sum_r a_rw^r\). With the prescribed factor \(2/\pi\), \hyperref[eq:20]{(20)} gives \(A_z g_v(z)\to\mathbb E_z[Sk_v]\), since \(\sin\varphi\) and the folded sign change together.

It remains to impose the interior value \(\psi_v(z)=v\) exactly on every approximating path. The correction below has a cost controlled by the same scalar \(A_z\) that appears in \hyperref[eq:20]{(20)}.

For \(0<t<1\) set \(p_t(w)=(z^{-1}+tz)w/(1+tw^2)\) and take \(\psi_v=g_v+(v-g_v(z))p_t\). Both endpoint conditions hold. On the circle,

\[
 \operatorname{Im}p_t(e^{i\varphi})
 =\frac{(z^{-1}+tz)(1-t)\sin\varphi}{(1-t)^2+4t\cos^2\varphi}.
\]

The denominator of \(p_t\) vanishes only at modulus \(1/\sqrt t>1\). \(p_t(0)=0\), \(p_t(z)=1\). Multiply numerator and denominator on the circle by \(1+te^{-2i\varphi}\): the imaginary numerator becomes \((z^{-1}+tz)(1-t)\sin\varphi\) and the squared denominator is \((1-t)^2+4t\cos^2\varphi\). Its sign is the sign of \(\sin\varphi\). Put \(S=|\sin\varphi|\). The discrepancy between the absolute value and its product with the signed sine is \(|\operatorname{Im}p_t|(1-S)\). Since \(1-S=\cos^2\varphi/(1+S)\), it is bounded uniformly by \((z^{-1}+tz)(1-t)/(4t)\), including at \(\cos\varphi=0\) by continuity. Thus its mean tends to zero although the path itself can be large near those angles. Formula \hyperref[eq:20]{(20)} for \(p_t\) holds by uniform convergence of the power series on the closed disk for each fixed \(t\). The mean absolute cost approaches the forced lower value \(A_z\).

For fixed \(t\) the corrected paths are analytic on a neighborhood of the closed unit disk and satisfy both endpoint conditions. Using the limit of the absolute cost in the allocation bound gives \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)}, as detailed below. The special choice \(g_v=0\) yields

\[
 \mathcal B_z\le f_0+QA_z=f_0+(\pi/2)Q\sinh\kappa.                       \tag{21}\label{eq:21}
\]

To track the order of approximations, fix the Fejér polynomials, then let \(t\uparrow1\) in the upper bound for the infimum. Positive homogeneity and evenness give \[
 H_*\big((\pi/2)\operatorname{Im}p_t\,(\mathbf v-\mathbf g(z))\big)
 =(\pi/2)|\operatorname{Im}p_t|\, H_*(\mathbf v-\mathbf g(z)).
\] Now take the Fejér limit and use the Lipschitz bounds. The correction costs \(H_*(A_z\mathbf v-\mathbb E_z[S\mathbf k])\). If its vector is increasing, inserting the ordered moments gives \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)}, with a minus sign before the gain. A zero field gives \hyperref[eq:21]{(21)} because \(A_z=(\pi/2)\sinh\kappa\). No limiting boundary function itself has to be an admissible analytic path.

\section{7. Combining the estimates and passing to all polynomials}\label{combining-the-estimates-and-passing-to-all-polynomials}

We now fix a degree ceiling and add the estimates for the very same \(R\) inside \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)}. Closeness at another conjugate can reduce the required closeness at the principal root. We compare the resulting loss with the improvement at that other conjugate. This comparison must hold for every allowed division of closeness among the roots.

Let \(C=w_d+1\). For the principal root use \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)} with a verified upper value \(\mathcal B\) and apply \hyperref[eq:21]{(21)} at other roots. Put \(g=A_0+G+f_0\), using certified upper values throughout. The numerical conditions we need are

\[
 F_B(C)> D+b\{d(A_0+G)+(d-1)f_0+\kappa s+\mathcal B\}.                  \tag{22}\label{eq:22}
\]

Here and below one may use \(B=bL\kappa_-/2\) with \(\kappa_-\le\kappa\), since \(F_B\) is increasing in \(B\). On the right an upper enclosure for \(\kappa\) is used. If \(2B/3\le c\le2B\), then

\[
 F_B(c)=B-\frac{(2B-c)^2}{8B},\qquad
 F'_B(c)=\frac{2B-c}{4B}.                                                 \tag{23}\label{eq:23}
\]

\textbf{Minimizing over all labels.} The simplex consists of all \(T+1\) nonnegative coordinates with sum one. The cost \(J(p)=B\sum_\ell p_\ell^2+c\sum_\ell\ell p_\ell\) has positive quadratic part \(B>0\). At a minimum the marginal costs \(a_\ell=2Bp_\ell+c\ell\) on positive coordinates have one common value \(\lambda\), since one can transfer a small amount of mass in either direction between them. At zero coordinates the marginal must be at least \(\lambda\), since one can transfer mass to such a coordinate. These conditions are sufficient: for any other feasible \(q\), \[
 J(q)-J(p)=\sum_\ell(a_\ell-\lambda)(q_\ell-p_\ell)+B\sum_\ell(q_\ell-p_\ell)^2\ge0 .
\] Testing positive mass only at \(0,1\) gives \(2Bp_0=2Bp_1+c\) and \(p_0+p_1=1\). Hence \(p_1=(2B-c)/(4B)\), nonnegative for \(c\le2B\), and \(\lambda=(2B+c)/2\). The cheapest unused index costs \(2c\), at least \(\lambda\) precisely when \(c\ge2B/3\). At equality an unused coordinate can have the same marginal without changing the minimizing point. Substitution gives \(J=B+(c-2B)p_1+2Bp_1^2=B-(2B-c)^2/(8B)\) and differentiation gives \hyperref[eq:23]{(23)} on the interval, with the indicated one-sided derivatives at its ends. This establishes the formula even if many higher labels are present.

Indeed \(p_1=(2B-c)/(4B)\), \(p_0=1-p_1\) have equal marginal cost \(2Bp_0=c+2Bp_1=(2B+c)/2\). All inactive coordinates \(\ell\ge2\) have cost at least \(2c\ge(2B+c)/2\). By convexity this is a minimum, including both endpoints. If \(2B/3<C-d+1<C<2B\), the loss from replacing \(C\) by \(C-y\), \(0\le y\le d-1\), is at most \(my\), where

\[
 m=\frac{2B-C+d-1}{4B},\qquad
 h_*=\frac{d-1}{bL},\qquad
 \gamma=\frac12-\frac{s}{L}-\frac{\pi Q}{2L(1-h_*^2)}.                  \tag{24}\label{eq:24}
\]

The derivative in \hyperref[eq:23]{(23)} decreases as \(c\) increases. Its largest value on \([C-d+1,C]\) is \(m\), so \(F_B(C)-F_B(C-y)=\int_{C-y}^C F'_B(t)\,dt\le my\). Compare this with the gain at a secondary root. To avoid conflict with the root size \(B_i\), call its quadratic cost parameter \(B^{\rm cost}=bL\kappa_i/2\). For \(\kappa_i=c_i/(bL)\) one has \(c_i=2B^{\rm cost}\). Here \(p_0=1\) minimizes and \(F_{B^{\rm cost}}(c_i)=c_i/2\).

Any secondary root with \(c_i>0\) tends to \(\pi\) and satisfies \(|\pi-\alpha_i|=\exp(-(c_i+o(1))X)\) because a vanishing normalized distance forces \(\alpha_i\) and hence \(B_i\) to be bounded. Apply \hyperref[eq:18]{(18)}, \hyperref[eq:21]{(21)} with \(\kappa_i=c_i/(bL)\). For this choice the cost just computed equals \(c_i/2\). Its gain over the ordinary length estimate \(bg\) is at least \(\gamma c_i\), since \(c_i/(bL)\le h_*<1\) and \(\sinh x\le x/(1-x^2)\) by the power series. For \(c_i=0\), \hyperref[eq:13]{(13)} bounds \(\log(|R(\alpha_i)|/B_i^{\deg R})/(nX)\) by \(bg+o(1)\). The same normalization can only improve the estimates for roots with \(c_i>0\).

In detail, \hyperref[eq:18]{(18)} and \hyperref[eq:21]{(21)} bound the secondary term by \[
 bg+b\kappa_i s+b(\pi/2)Q\sinh\kappa_i-c_i/2 .
\] Termwise comparison with the geometric series gives \(\sinh t\le t/(1-t^2)\) for \(0\le t<1\). As \(\kappa_i\le h_*\), the two positive additions are at most \(c_i[s/L+\pi Q/(2L(1-h_*^2))]\). The net saving is at least \(\gamma c_i\). When \(c_i=0\) the elementary bound \(|R(\alpha_i)|\le\mathcal L(R)B_i^{\deg R}\) suffices. When \(c_i>0\), division by \(B_i^{\deg R}\ge1\) only decreases the upper estimate.

Suppose \(\sum c_i\ge C\) and put \(y=\sum_{i>1}c_i\). By \hyperref[eq:4]{(4)}, \(y\le d-1\) and \(c_1\ge C-y>0\). The latter finite threshold suffices in \hyperref[eq:18]{(18)}, also if \(c_1=+\infty\). Apply \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)}, use \(\log\mathcal M(f)\le X\), and bound the \(e-1\) secondary terms as above. The upper limit of \(\log|\operatorname{Res}(f,R)|/(nX)\) is at most

\[
 D+b\{d(A_0+G)+(d-1)f_0+\kappa s+\mathcal B\}
       -F_B(C)+(m-\gamma)y .                                              \tag{25}\label{eq:25}
\]

Replacing \(e\) by \(d\) here is valid because \(g\ge0\). If \hyperref[eq:22]{(22)} holds and \(\gamma\ge m\), \hyperref[eq:25]{(25)} is strictly negative. This contradicts \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)}. Hence no such root sequence exists.

The term \(D\) in \hyperref[eq:25]{(25)} pays for \(\deg R\,\log\mathcal M(f)/(nX)\). The ordinary cost \(bg\) is allowed for each secondary root, with \(\gamma y\) subtracted. The principal estimate uses \(c=C-y\) and loses at most \(my\) relative to \(c=C\). Thus the largest upper bound on \(0\le y\le d-1\) occurs at \(y=0\) when \(\gamma\ge m\). This identifies the worst case for the estimate without assuming that other roots stay far away. A strictly negative upper limit implies \(|\operatorname{Res}(f,R)|<1\) for all sufficiently large members of the proposed sequence. Each member already has a nonzero Gaussian integer as its resultant.

To recover the endpoint constant, if \(\inf_f H(f)^{C-1}|f(\pi)|=0\) over the primitive irreducibles of degree at most \(d\), choose a sequence along which the product tends to zero. A bounded-height subset is finite, and no such polynomial vanishes at \(\pi\) by its transcendence (also a consequence of the Hermite--Lindemann theorem applied to \(i\pi\) {[}1{]}). Thus \(X\to\infty\) on a subsequence. Since \(\log H(f)=X+O_d(1)\), \hyperref[eq:4]{(4)} would give \(\sum c_i\ge C\). This proves a uniform positive lower constant for all those irreducibles.

In particular the argument excludes even a sequence for which \(H(f)^{w_d}|f(\pi)|\) tends to zero slower than any negative power of \(H(f)\). Indeed eventually \(-\log|f(\pi)|\ge w_d\log H(f)\) and \(\log H(f)/X\to1\). Formula \hyperref[eq:4]{(4)} gives \(\sum c_i-1\ge w_d\), with equality allowed in the hypothesized violation. The strict inequality in \hyperref[eq:22]{(22)} excludes equality here. For a single polynomial this reasoning would not force its height to grow. The fixed-input nonvanishing proved in C.1 is what allows the extraction of an unbounded sequence.

Finally write \(P=h\prod_{k=1}^r f_k\) with \(r\le d\), counting multiplicity, and \(h\) a nonzero integer. By \hyperref[eq:3]{(3)} the inequality for each \(f_k\) implies \(|f_k(\pi)|\ge c'_d\mathcal M(f_k)^{-w_d}\) with \(0<c'_d\le1\). Multiply, using \(|h|\ge1\), \(\mathcal M(P)=|h|\prod_k\mathcal M(f_k)\) and \(\mathcal M(P)\le(d+1)H(P)\). One may take \((c'_d)^d(d+1)^{-w_d}\) for \(c_d\), also for a constant polynomial. This completes the proof of Theorem 1 subject only to the finite inequalities in Section 8.

For the constants in this last step, write \(c>0\) for the irreducible lower constant, and take \(c'_d=\min(1,c\,2^{-dw_d})\). From \(H(f)\le2^e\mathcal M(f)\) it follows that \(|f(\pi)|\ge c'_d\mathcal M(f)^{-w_d}\). Gauss's lemma factors an integral polynomial into its integer content and primitive irreducibles over \(\mathbb Q\): the product of primitive polynomials is primitive because reduction modulo any prime gives a product of nonzero polynomials over a field. Clearing denominators in a rational factorization now proves the assertion. Counting repeated factors individually gives \(r\le\deg P\), and \[
 |P(\pi)|\ge |h|(c'_d)^r\prod_k\mathcal M(f_k)^{-w_d}
 = |h|^{1+w_d}(c'_d)^r\mathcal M(P)^{-w_d}
 \ge (c'_d)^d(d+1)^{-w_d} H(P)^{-w_d}.
\] For a constant, \(r=0\) and the empty product equals one. In particular coefficient cancellation on multiplication of factors creates no problem: their Mahler measures multiply exactly.

\section{8. Parameters and numerical inequalities}\label{parameters-and-numerical-inequalities}

The proof now asks for two examples of a shape and a boundary field satisfying strict inequalities. A search can suggest these examples, but only the rational input and its verified bounds enter the proof. Read the first table as a budget of individual costs and the second as a check that their combination leaves a positive surplus.

For both cases take the masses \(a_v=Lr_{|v|}/(r_0+2\sum_{h=1}^V r_h)\). The following lists are integers, in order from \(j=0\) or \(|v|=0\). They specify all bands and masses exactly.

\[
\begin{aligned}
 d=2:\quad 10^4\mathbf u={}&(10000,9408,8756,8080,7358,\\
 &6574,5744,5460,4484,3256),\\
 \mathbf r={}&(10000,9434,8482,6139).
\end{aligned}
\] \[
\begin{aligned}
 d=3:\quad 10^4\mathbf u={}&(10000,9745,9400,9044,8686,8318,\\
 &7940,7547,7140,6714,6260,6122,\\
 &5634,5096,4504,3834,3004,1920),\\
 \mathbf r={}&(10000,9812,9657,9107,8129,6640,3684).
\end{aligned}
\]

The lengths are \(L=1728/125\) and \(30227/1250\). For \(J<T\), the smallest selection slack \(B_J-2\sum_{j\le J}u_j\) is respectively \(407/625\) and \(48/125\). The last slack is exactly zero in both cases. Thus Lemma 3.1 applies. \textbf{Table 1} gives exact \(b,z,C\) and upper bounds for the remaining quantities, using \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)} for \(\mathcal B\).

To obtain \(Q\), the positive cut \(x\) for an upper tail of mass \(a\) solves \(\sum_j(u_j-x)_+=a\) and the tail's first moment is \(\frac12\sum_j(u_j^2-x^2)_+\). Both are rational. To obtain \(G\), integrate each positive part of an affine function in \hyperref[eq:9]{(9)}. For endpoints \(l<r\) with respective values \(u>0>v\), its positive integral is \((r-l)u^2/(2(u-v))\). The roles of the endpoints may be interchanged. These two calculations require no numerical quadrature.

For \hyperref[eq:22]{(22)} the lower bound for \(F_B(C)\) and the upper bound for its right side are respectively \(12.5501790262\) and \(12.5501598073\) in degree two, \(21.6842515238\) and \(21.6842204255\) in degree three. The following strict inequalities come from the unrounded enclosures.
