\textbf{Table 2.} The surplus in the principal-root inequality and the slopes for other roots. In both cases \(g>0\), \(0<h_*<1\), and \(2B/3<C-d+1<C<2B\). The smallest of the adjacent differences \(d_v-d_{v-1}\) in \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)} exceeds \(0.00004999\). These checks establish all the assumptions used in \hyperref[eq:25]{(25)}, and prove \hyperref[eq:1]{(1)}.

For a concrete arithmetic check, in degree two \(\sum_j u_j=6.912\) and \(2\sum_j u_j=13.824=1728/125\). There are ten layers (\(T=9\)) and seven nodes (\(V=3\)). Degree three has eighteen layers (\(T=17\)) and thirteen nodes (\(V=6\)). Each \(B_J\) is computed by sorting the masses \emph{within each residue} and taking at most \(J+1\) from each class. At \(J=T\) all masses have been taken and their sum equals \(L\). In computing \hyperref[eq:9]{(9)}, fix \(h\) and split the \(y\)-interval at every internal point \(a_v/(h-q_v)\) or \(b_j/(h-j)\) with positive denominator. Between these cuts \(R_h-C_h\) is affine. An additional crossing is integrated by the triangle formula above, with no sampling assumption. For one additional check on \hyperref[eq:8]{(8)}, in degree two the largest tested loss is \(55/288\) at \(\zeta=359/625\), and in degree three it is \(186896039/1048453722\) at \(\zeta=313/500\). The comparison in \hyperref[eq:22]{(22)} uses directed bounds before table rounding. In particular \(f_0\) is a negative upper bound and enters with the sign printed in \hyperref[eq:22]{(22)}.

\section{Appendix A. Finite certificates for the space and angle variables}\label{appendix-a.-finite-certificates-for-the-space-and-angle-variables}

In \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)} the active node can change between two adjacent sample points. In \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)} a path correction depends on an integral over arcs of the circle. A finite certificate must enclose both integrals over their full domains. We give the error estimate and substitutions explicitly, then trace one entry through the degree-two witness.

\subsection{A.1. Witness data}\label{a.1.-witness-data}

The accompanying archive contains one JSON input for each degree. Each gives the integer bands and row weights of Section 8, the exact \(b,C,z\), and all reference fields actually used. A field contains one vector \((k_v)\), charges \((\beta_v)\) and 80 equal-width bins of reference density on \([-1,1]\) for \(0\le v\le V\). All three arrays are integer numerators in units of \(10^{-5}\). For negative \(v\), reflect the density in \(x\), put \(\beta_{-v}=\beta_v\) and \(k_{-v}=-k_v\). The zero-node density is even. There are 54 and 50 reference fields, respectively, including the zero-field witness for \(f_0\). The charges and reference densities in \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)} are arbitrary, so the verification does not depend on how the inputs were found.

The data play three different roles. The masses \(a_v\) are prescribed by the determinant. The densities \(\rho_v\) occur only in an upper bound and need not share those masses. The vector \(\mathbf k\) is the boundary field for which that upper bound is computed. A zero field means that every \(k_v=0\). Its reference densities and charges can still be nonzero. The same index \(v\) in the three arrays allows the pointwise maximum in \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)} to be evaluated node by node.

\subsection{A.2. Integrating a reference field}\label{a.2.-integrating-a-reference-field}

Set

\[
 F(x)=x(\log(2|x|)-1),\qquad J(x)=\tfrac12x^2\log(2|x|)-\tfrac34x^2,
 \qquad F(0)=J(0)=0 .
\]

Then \(J'=F\) and \(F'(x)=\log(2|x|)\) off the origin. A constant density \(r\) on \([l,h]\) contributes \(r(F(x-l)-F(x-h))\) to its potential. A pair of unit densities on \([a,b],[c,d]\) contributes \(J(b-c)-J(a-c)-J(b-d)+J(a-d)\) to \(I\). Thus the only spatial integral in \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)} still needing enclosure is the pointwise maximum.

For example, first integrate in \(y\in[c,d]\) to obtain \(F(x-c)-F(x-d)\), then integrate in \(x\in[a,b]\). The logarithmic singularity is integrable and \(F\) is continuous at zero, so splitting the intervals at a singularity justifies the primitive calculation even when the intervals overlap. Writing a density as its jumps at grid edges gives \(U_{\rho_v}(x)=\sum_h\Delta\rho_v(h)F(x-h)\) by telescoping adjacent bin contributions.

Take mesh width \(\delta=1/10000\) on \([-1,1]\). All density jumps and band endpoints lie on it. Estimate the integral of the envelope in \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)} by the weighted trapezoid sum of its endpoint maxima. The following number is an \textbf{additive error bound for the integral over the entire interval} \([-1,1]\). It allows the maximizing node to change arbitrarily often within a mesh cell.

\[
 (T+1)\left(\sum_h\max_v|\Delta\rho_v(h)|\right)
 \delta^2\left(1+\frac{1+\lceil\log_2(2/\delta)\rceil}{4}\right).           \tag{26}\label{eq:26}
\]

Here \(\Delta\rho_v(h)\) is the signed jump of the piecewise constant density at edge \(h\), including both ends against zero. Indeed \(U_{\rho_v}=\sum_h\Delta\rho_v(h)F(x-h)\), and the density of \(\nu\) is bounded by \(T+1\). Taking the maximum before this sum of absolute error bounds covers changes of active node inside a cell. The full envelope is even, so its trapezoid sum is twice the one on \([0,1]\).

\textbf{An envelope over a whole mesh cell.} Let \(I\) be one cell \([l,l+\delta]\), and let \(\mathcal I\) denote linear interpolation between its endpoints. Put \(g_v(x)=k_vx+U_{\rho_v}(x)-\beta_v\) and \(g=\max_v g_v\). If \(x=l+t\delta\), \(0\le t\le1\), then \[
 \mathcal I g_v(x)=(1-t)g_v(l)+t g_v(l+\delta)\le\mathcal I g(x).
\] At each \(x\), even if a different \(v\) attains the maximum there, \[
 g(x)\le\mathcal I g(x)+\sum_h\max_v|\Delta\rho_v(h)|
                   \,|F(x-h)-\mathcal I[F(\,\cdot-h)](x)|.
\] The linear and constant terms interpolate exactly. Only this single interpolation of endpoint maxima is needed, once the remaining sum has been allowed for. On the cell immediately to the right of \(h\), writing \(x-h=t\delta\) gives the exact residual \(\delta t\log t\). Oddness gives the same absolute integral on the left. Each is \(\delta^2/4\), and using \(\delta^2\) for their joint cost is safe. On a cell at distance at least \(j\delta>0\), \(F''(u)=1/u\) gives the interpolation bound \(|F-\mathcal IF|\le (\delta^2/8)/(j\delta)\). Indeed the second-derivative interpolation remainder has the factor \((x-l)(x-l-\delta)/2\) by two applications of Rolle's theorem. Integrate over the cell to get \(\delta^2/(8j)\). At most two cells of each distance index occur, one on either side. Grouping the harmonic series into dyadic intervals gives \(\sum_{j\le m}1/j\le1+\lceil\log_2 m\rceil\). Multiply the resulting sum by the jump maxima and the upper density \(T+1\) to obtain \hyperref[eq:26]{(26)}. This estimate includes the edges at \(-1\) and \(1\) against zero. On each open cell the actual density of \(\nu\) is constant, so the chord integrates to its trapezoid exactly with that cell weight. No regularity of the maximizing index is required.

For the logarithms of mesh differences, add consecutive increments

\[
 \log(k+1)-\log k=2\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{1}{(2j+1)(2k+1)^{2j+1}}.
\]

In units of \(10^{-23}\) round each retained summand outward. Once the next term is less than one unit, the tail is less than two, by the ratio bound \((2k+1)^{-2}\le1/9\). The resulting primitive tables enclose \(F\) and \(J\) with absolute errors at most two units on scales \(10^9\) and \(10^{12}\) respectively. Signed products use absolute coefficients in their error budgets. A four-corner expression for \(J\) costs at most eight units times the appropriate product of density norms. Integer matrix products are tested against their signed 64-bit ranges. Outer sums and rational arithmetic use arbitrary-precision integers. These rules explain the one-sided values supplied to \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)}.

For the primitive tables at \(k\delta>0\) use \(\log(2k\delta)=\log2+\log k-\log10000\) and the explicit definitions of \(F,J\). The logarithmic increment series comes from integrating \(2/(1-t^2)\) to \(1/(2k+1)\). If its first omitted positive term is smaller than one unit of \(10^{-23}\), all omitted terms have sum less than \(1/(1-1/9)<2\) units. The table checks compare the lower rounded primitive plus two storage units against the independently propagated upper bound for each \(k\). Oddness of \(F\) and evenness of \(J\) handle negative arguments. At one mesh point, the potential error is bounded by \(2\sum_h|\Delta\rho_v(h)|\) in first-primitive units. For one reference energy the cost is \(8(\sum_i|r_i|)^2\) in second-primitive units when the bin values are \(r_i\). Dividing by the density scales and by \(L\) gives the enclosure of \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)}. The sign of \(-\frac12 I\) calls for the lower end of the energy interval.

\subsection{A.3. Exact angular masses}\label{a.3.-exact-angular-masses}

For \(\varphi\in[0,\pi/2]\) put \(y=\tan(\varphi/2)\), \(r=(1+z)/(1-z)\) and \(c=(1-y^2)/(1+y^2)\). Let \(W(y)\) denote cumulative folded Poisson mass and \(J_z(y)\) cumulative folded integral of \(S\). Addition of the four reflected densities yields

\[
 \begin{aligned}
 W(y)&=\frac2\pi\big(\arctan(ry)+\arctan(y/r)\big),\\
 J_z(y)&=\frac{1-z^2}{2\pi z}\left(2\log r-
            \log\frac{1+z^2+2zc}{1+z^2-2zc}\right).
 \end{aligned}                                                            \tag{27}\label{eq:27}
\]

For example the folded density per \(d\varphi\) is \((1-z^2)\pi^{-1}[(1-2zc+z^2)^{-1}+(1+2zc+z^2)^{-1}]\). With \(d\varphi=2dy/(1+y^2)\) its integral is \(W\). Multiplication by \(\sin\varphi\) and substitution \(dc=-\sin\varphi\,d\varphi\) give \(J_z\), with both primitives zero at \(y=0\). Break \([0,1]\) into 256 equal cells and form the stated vector on each cell by convex combinations of the reference fields with positive weights in units of \(10^{-8}\) summing to one. By convexity, the same combination of certified spatial upper bounds bounds the mixed field. Equal neighboring choices merge into 57 intervals for \(d=2\) and 55 for \(d=3\).

To derive the first primitive, the four original angles \(\varphi,\pi-\varphi,\pi+\varphi,2\pi-\varphi\) have cosines \(c,-c,-c,c\). Folding therefore gives two copies of each denominator. Under the tangent half-angle substitution, \[
 1\mp2zc+z^2=\frac{(1\mp z)^2+(1\pm z)^2y^2}{1+y^2}.
\] Multiplying by \(d\varphi=2\,dy/(1+y^2)\), the two summands of the folded measure become \((2/\pi)r\,dy/(1+r^2y^2)\) and \((2/\pi)r^{-1}\,dy/(1+y^2/r^2)\). Integration from zero gives \(W\). In particular \(W(1)=1\) because \(\arctan r+\arctan(1/r)=\pi/2\).

For the sine-weighted integral put \(a=1+z^2\) and substitute \(u=\cos\varphi\). We obtain \[
 J_z(y)=\frac{1-z^2}{\pi}\int_c^1
 \left(\frac1{a-2zu}+\frac1{a+2zu}\right)\,du .
\] A primitive is \((2z)^{-1}\log((a+2zu)/(a-2zu))\). At \(u=1\) its logarithm is \(\log((1+z)^2/(1-z)^2)=2\log r\). Subtracting the lower value gives \hyperref[eq:27]{(27)} with the required sign. Both cumulative integrals are zero at the left endpoint.

For an interval \([l,r']\) let \(\Delta W=W(r')-W(l)\), \(\Delta J=J_z(r')-J_z(l)\), and let \(f\) bound \(\mathcal F(\mathbf k)\). Add \(\Delta J\sum_v k_vm_v^*/L-\Delta W f\) to the gain subtracted in \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)}. For each \(v>0\) add \(\Delta J(k_v-k_{v-1})\) to its normalization expense. Independent interval endpoints are used when subtracting, and multiplication by a negative rational reverses the endpoints. Compare all expenses with a lower bound for \(A_z\). Finally evaluate \hyperref[eq:22]{(22)}--\hyperref[eq:24]{(24)} with directed intervals. Logarithms outside the mesh use the series \(2\sum_{j\ge0}t^{2j+1}/(2j+1)\), \(t=(x-1)/(x+1)\) after power-of-two reduction to \(1\le x\le2\). A geometric tail encloses the remainder. The alternating arctangent series after reduction to \([0,1/2]\), together with \(\pi=16\arctan(1/5)-4\arctan(1/239)\), supplies the other endpoints.

As a numerical trace in degree two, the rightmost node has mass \(1768032/1210625\). Its left quantile is \(86128/145275\), between \(u_6\) and \(u_5\), and solves \(\sum_{j=0}^5(u_j-x)=1768032/1210625\). The first angular interval is \(0\le y\le2/256\), assigned the zero field. Its contribution to the lower gain in \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)} is \(-\Delta W f_0>0\) although every direction expense on it is zero. This also tests the sign of the energy term.

If a cell mixes fields \(\mathbf k^{(i)}\) with weights \(\lambda_i\ge0\) adding to one, the linear moment term uses the mixed vector while convexity gives \(\mathcal F(\sum_i\lambda_i\mathbf k^{(i)})\le\sum_i\lambda_i f_i\). Add the lower end of the resulting gain to bound the subtracted expectation from below. For the direction test compute \(d_v-d_{v-1}=A_z-\mathbb E_z[S(k_v-k_{v-1})]\). A positive lower endpoint is sufficient. Symmetry supplies the corresponding negative-node tests. For the elementary angle bounds, \(\arctan x=\int_0^x(1+t^2)^{-1}dt\) yields its alternating series and an error bounded by the first omitted term. The identities \(\arctan x=\pi/2-\arctan(1/x)\) for \(x>1\) and \(\arctan x=\pi/4-\arctan((1-x)/(1+x))\) for \(1/2<x\le1\) reduce the arguments. The stated identity for \(\pi\) follows by applying the tangent doubling and subtraction formulas to \(4\arctan(1/5)-\arctan(1/239)\), whose tangent is \(1\) and whose value lies in \((0,\pi/2)\). These bounds use rational arithmetic with endpoint directions preserved. In a subtraction \([A,B]-[C,D]=[A-D,B-C]\). In particular a negative field bound in a \emph{subtracted} term can contribute a positive lower gain.

The supplementary archive includes the input arrays, verifier and a machine-readable record of all final rational intervals. From the extracted directory run \texttt{python\ verify.py} using Python 3.10 or later with NumPy installed. To write a fresh record run \texttt{python\ verify.py\ -\/-json\ results.json}. The program evaluates the raw spatial fields, arithmetic profiles, angular intervals and both transfers. The supplied output record is unnecessary for a replay.

Appendix D explains how to retrieve the verification archive embedded in this PDF. The integer weights specifying angular mixtures have denominator \(10^8\), distinct from the denominator \(10^5\) of field and density entries.

\section{Appendix B. A separate estimate when the degree grows}\label{appendix-b.-a-separate-estimate-when-the-degree-grows}

A fixed collection of bands need not provide an estimate uniform in the degree ceiling. We now use a rectangle of frequencies and derivative layers, with explicit dependence on \(d\). The determinant will be compared at a single close real root, first by a resultant lower bound and then by analytic interpolation. There is a positive rational margin after both bounds are divided by the same scale.

We prove \hyperref[eq:2]{(2)} to specify exactly what carries over to growing degrees. Write \(\mathscr D=d\log d\). This appendix uses new parameters

\[
 a=33/8,\quad b=3/5,\quad c=6/7,\quad s=511/125,\quad C=208/25 .
\]

For a real algebraic root \(\alpha\ne0\) of a primitive irreducible \(f\) of degree at most \(d\), put \(X=\log\mathcal L(f)\) and suppose \(|\alpha-\pi|<e^{-C\mathscr D X}\). Set

\[
 \begin{gathered}
 T=\lfloor a\mathscr D\rfloor,\quad V=\lfloor c\mathscr D\rfloor,\quad
 U=\lfloor bX\rfloor,\quad S=\lfloor sX\rfloor,\quad h=\lfloor X/\mathscr D\rfloor,\\
 H=2U+1,\qquad M=2V+1,\qquad n=(T+1)H .
 \end{gathered}
\]

Write \(o_*(1)\) for a quantity whose absolute upper limit as \(X\to\infty\) goes to zero when \(d\to\infty\). All estimates below use this order. In particular \(h\ge1\) for large \(X\) at fixed \(d\).

Thus for any tolerance \(\epsilon>0\) a residual \(o_*(1)\) can be made smaller than \(\epsilon\) for every sufficiently large \(d\), then for all \(X\) beyond a threshold depending on that \(d\) (enlarging thresholds to absorb a limsup). In particular \(T,V\) are fixed during each height limit, while \(U,S,h\) grow with \(X\). A constant depending only on \(d\), divided by \(X\), disappears in the first limit even if that constant grows quickly with \(d\).

\subsection{B.1. Selecting low derivatives}\label{b.1.-selecting-low-derivatives}

Put \(B_l(x)=x(x+1)\cdots(x+l-1)/l!\), \(B_0=1\), and \(E_\sigma=B_h^{\lfloor\sigma/h\rfloor}B_{\sigma\bmod h}\). All are integer-valued on the integers. For every \(|t|\le U\) and \(0\le j\le T\) form a column. For \(|v|\le V\), \(0\le\sigma\le S\), take the entries

\[
 d_j i^{vt}(\partial_t+ivY/2)^jE_\sigma(t),\qquad
 d_j=(2\,\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,h))^j .                              \tag{28}\label{eq:28}
\]

Choose independent rows at \(\alpha\) in nondecreasing order of \(\sigma\). On a run of \(q\) consecutive frequencies keep all \(T+1\) layers. The columns restricted to orders \(0,\ldots,k\) have full rank \((T+1)q\) if \((k+1)M>(T+M)q\). We use here the classical multiplicity lemma {[}1, Lemma 2{]}. At \(M\) points \((x_v,y_v)\) with distinct \(x_v\) and \(y_v\ne0\), a nonzero polynomial of bidegree at most \((T,q-1)\) cannot vanish with all derivatives of orders \(0,\ldots,k\) for \(\partial_x+y\partial_y\) under that strict inequality. To apply it take \((x_v,y_v)=(iv\alpha/2,i^v)\). A dependence of columns gives exactly that polynomial after shifting the lowest frequency to zero by a monomial in \(y\). Along \((x_v+w,y_ve^w)\) the monomial is a nonvanishing factor. The polynomials \(E_0,\ldots,E_k\) form a triangular basis of the operators of orders at most \(k\).

To see the operator statement, a row equation on a putative relation is \(E_\sigma(\partial_w)\) evaluated at \(w=0\) on the exponential polynomial along \((x_v+w,y_ve^w)\). The identity before \hyperref[eq:16]{(16)} proves this, since the exponential factor at \(w=0\) supplies the phase \(y_v^t\) and the \(w\)-dependent exponential is \(e^{tw}\). The nonzero \(d_j\) are absorbed in the column coefficients. Each \(E_\sigma\) has degree \(\sigma\) with nonzero leading coefficient, so the operators for \(\sigma\le k\) span \(1,\partial_w,\ldots,\partial_w^k\). Shifting the least possibly negative frequency to zero multiplies the function along the trajectory by an exponential with nonzero constant term and preserves its order. The resulting bidegree bounds are \(T\) in \(x\), \(q-1\) in \(y\). The \(x_v\) are distinct since \(\alpha\ne0\), including when \(\alpha\) is complex.

Write a putative nonzero polynomial as \(\sum_{j=1}^K Q_j(x)y^{m_j}\), with \(K\le q\), distinct \(m_j\) and nonzero \(Q_j\) of degrees at most \(T\). Take the Wronskian of the functions \(Q_j(x)e^{m_jx}\). Removing \(e^{m_jx}\) from column \(j\) leaves the polynomial \[
 W_*(x)=\det\big((\partial_x+m_j)^r Q_j(x)\big)_{0\le r<K,\ 1\le j\le K}.
\]

\textbf{The multiplicity count in detail.} For the nonzero terms of the shifted polynomial let \(b_j\) and \(d'_j\) be the leading coefficient and degree of \(Q_j\). After the exponentials are removed, column \(j\) has degree at most \(d'_j\). Its top-degree coefficient in row \(r\) is \(b_jm_j^r\), with \(m_j^0=1\). This also describes the zero coefficient when \(m_j=0\) and \(r>0\). The coefficient of degree \(\sum_j d'_j\) in \(W_*\) is therefore \((\prod b_j)\prod_{i<j}(m_j-m_i)\ne0\). The determinant is a polynomial of degree at most \(KT\). At \(x=x_v+w\) the function \(\sum_j y_v^{m_j} e^{-m_jx_v}Q_j(x)e^{m_jx}\) is exactly the polynomial restricted to the trajectory. Every coefficient in the column replacement is nonzero because \(y_v\ne0\). Replacing any one column by the indicated combination changes the Wronskian by a nonzero constant, and factoring out exponentials changes the zero order by zero. The function has order at least \(k+1\). Its derivatives up to \(K-1\) have orders at least \(k+1-(K-1)=k+2-K\) when this is positive. Thus at \(M\) distinct points the determinant would have at least \(M(k+2-K)_+\) zeros in total. The assumed strict inequality implies \[
 M(k+2-K)>(T+M)q+M(1-K)=Tq+M(q+1-K)\ge KT
\] because \(K\le q\). This is incompatible with a nonzero polynomial of degree at most \(KT\) over \(\mathbb C\). All order comparisons in this proof are local Taylor-order comparisons. They require no ordering of complex points.

Thus by \(k_q=\lfloor(T+M)q/M\rfloor\) the whole scan has chosen at least \((T+1)q\) independent rows. For large \(d\) then large \(X\) it reaches \(n\) by \(S\), as \(s-b(a/c+2)=1/2000>0\). Let \(R^0\) denote the selected determinant in \hyperref[eq:28]{(28)}. It satisfies \(R^0(\alpha)\ne0\) and its mean selected order is bounded by

\[
 \bar\sigma\le H^{-1}\sum_{q=1}^H k_q
 \le (T+M)(H+1)/(2M)\le (s/2+o_*(1))X .                                 \tag{29}\label{eq:29}
\]

In fact \(k_q+1>(T+M)q/M\). Rank of the full matrix up to this order is at least that of its \((T+1)q\) restricted columns. In the greedy list, selected rows numbered \((q-1)(T+1)+1\) through \(q(T+1)\) consequently have order at most \(k_q\). Summing yields the first inequality of \hyperref[eq:29]{(29)}. \(\sum_{q=1}^Hq=H(H+1)/2\) gives the second. At fixed \(d\) the limit of \(k_H/X\) is \(2b(T+M)/M\). As \(d\to\infty\), \(T/\mathscr D\to a\) and \(M/\mathscr D\to2c\), giving \(2b+ab/c=s-1/2000\). The strict margin ensures \(k_H\le S\) for all sufficiently large \(d\) and then all sufficiently large \(X\). The corresponding average limit is at most \(s/2\). This argument does not require a prefix at each node in Appendix B.

\subsection{B.2. Integer lower bound}\label{b.2.-integer-lower-bound}

If \(\Delta\) is the forward difference, the identity for \(\partial=\log(1+\Delta)\) in Lemma 4.2 shows that \(\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,h)\partial\) preserves integer-valuedness for polynomials of degree at most \(h\). The product rule on the factors of \(E_\sigma\) gives integrality of \hyperref[eq:28]{(28)}, including both components at \(p=2\). For each odd \(p>h\) the coefficients of \(E_\sigma\) are already \(p\)-integral. In each fixed \(j\) and residue class, with \(H_l\) frequencies, take the \(k\)th column difference as in Section 4. Each entry is now a polynomial in the frequency index with \(p\)-integral coefficients, because the phase is constant. Its \(k\)th difference at zero is divisible by \(k!\), as one sees by expanding powers into falling factorials using integer Stirling numbers. Hence \(K=\big(\prod_l\prod_{k<H_l}k!_{>h}\big)^{T+1}\) divides every coefficient of \(R^0\), where \(k!_{>h}\) retains the odd primes larger than \(h\).

Here \(B_l(t)=\binom{t+l-1}l\) is integer-valued also at negative integers by the identities in Section 4. Put \(L_h=\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,h)\). On each factor of degree at most \(h\), iterating \(L_h\partial_t\) preserves integer-valuedness. In a derivative of the product, distribute its \(r\) differentiations among the factors with integer multinomial coefficients. \(L_h^r\) suffices for every term. On expansion of \hyperref[eq:28]{(28)} the coefficient with \(r\) derivatives also contains \(2^j/2^{j-r}=2^r\) and \(L_h^j\), so its value at an integer frequency is Gaussian integral. For \(p>h\) odd the factorial denominators of the factors of \(E_\sigma\) have no \(p\). After removing the phase, replacing \(t\) by \(t_l+4i'\) leaves a polynomial in the integer index \(i'\) with \(p\)-integral coefficients (coefficientwise in \(Y\)). The identity \(x(x)_{\underline r}=(x)_{\underline{r+1}}+r(x)_{\underline r}\) expands every monomial into falling factorials with integer coefficients. Applying \(\Delta^k\) at \(0\) picks out \(k!\) times its coefficient of \((x)_{\underline k}\). Thus the \(k\)th difference column is divisible at these primes by the indicated part of \(k!\). Taking the product over columns gives \(K\) without altering the determinant by the triangular change of columns.

In each group of identical \((\sigma,v\bmod4)\) divided differences yield \(Y^{\binom{m}{2}}\) for a group of \(m\) rows. There are at most \(4(S+1)\) groups. Cauchy--Schwarz therefore gives a total origin order \(l_0\ge n^2/(8(S+1))-n/2\). Thus \(R=R^0/(KY^{l_0})\in\mathbb Z[i][Y]\) with degree at most \(nT/2-l_0\) (take \(l_0\) to be the sum of the group orders). Its resultant with \(f\) is nonzero. By \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)}, \hyperref[eq:3]{(3)}, and the length bound on the remaining conjugates,

\[
 \log|R^0(\alpha)|\ge
 d\log K-(d-1)\log\mathcal L(R^0)-(nT/2-l_0)X .                          \tag{30}\label{eq:30}
\]

Here \(|\alpha|>1\) eventually. Its \(l_0\) factor was dropped, and \(\mathcal L(R^0)/K=\mathcal L(R)\ge1\) permits replacing \(\deg f\) by \(d\).

A group of \(m\) rows uses divided differences of orders \(0,\ldots,m-1\), giving the sum \(\binom m2\) even for this nonprefix selection because the rows of the group have the same \(\sigma\). Write \(l_0=\sum\binom m2\) over the groups. Since \(\sum m=n\), Cauchy--Schwarz yields \(\sum m^2\ge n^2/[4(S+1)]\). Also the full rectangular sum of the column degrees is \(H\sum_{j=0}^Tj=nT/2\). Dividing by \(Y^{l_0}\) shifts the coefficients, and dividing by the positive integer \(K\) preserves integrality by the column proof. For \(r=\deg R\), \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)} gives \[
 1\le \mathcal M(f)^r
       \frac{|R(\alpha)|}{\max(1,|\alpha|)^r}\,
       \mathcal L(R)^{e-1}.
\] Now substitute \(|R(\alpha)|=|R^0(\alpha)|/(K|\alpha|^{l_0})\), \(\mathcal L(R)=\mathcal L(R^0)/K\), and \(\mathcal M(f)\le e^X\). Both root-size factors that move to the lower bound are at least one for \(|\alpha|>1\). One obtains \(\log K-(e-1)\log(\mathcal L(R^0)/K)-rX\). Replacing \(e\) by \(d\) weakens this lower bound since \(\mathcal L(R)\ge1\). Replacing \(r\) by its upper bound gives \hyperref[eq:30]{(30)}.

We give the rates in \hyperref[eq:30]{(30)}. Since \(\bar k=\sum_lH_l(H_l-1)/(2H)=U/4+O(1)\), Legendre's factorial count with \(\lfloor k/p\rfloor\ge k/p-1\) gives \(\log K/n\ge\bar k\sum_{h<p\le U}(\log p)/p-\vartheta(U)\). At fixed \(d\), \(U/h\to b\mathscr D\). By partial summation of the prime number theorem the sum tends to \(\log(b\mathscr D)\). Thus \(d\log K/(n\mathscr D X)\ge b/4-o_*(1)\).

Legendre's count is \(v_p(k!)=\sum_{j\ge1}\lfloor k/p^j\rfloor\), obtained by counting multiples of each \(p^j\) among \(1,\ldots,k\). Keeping the first layer for \(h<p\le U\) yields the lower bound in the text even for \(p>k\), since \(k/p-1\le0\) then. Each of the four class lengths is \(H/4+O(1)\), so the mean difference index is \(\bar k=H/8+O(1)=U/4+O(1)\). Partial summation here has an explicit form \[
 \sum_{h<p\le U}\frac{\log p}{p}
 =\frac{\vartheta(U)}U-\frac{\vartheta(h)}h+\int_h^U\frac{\vartheta(t)}{t^2}\,dt .
\] It follows by integrating the step function \(\vartheta\) against \(1/t\) by parts. At fixed sufficiently large \(d\), \(U/h\to b\mathscr D>1\). Hence \(\sup_{t\ge h}|\vartheta(t)/t-1|\to0\) and \(\log(U/h)\) is bounded as \(X\to\infty\). The endpoint difference tends to zero and the integral is \(\log(b\mathscr D)+o(1)\) at that fixed \(d\). Upon division by \(n\mathscr D X\) and multiplication by \(d\), the surviving term is \((b/4)\log(b\mathscr D)/\log d\). The loss from \(\vartheta(U)\) is \(O(1/\log d)\). Since \(\log(bd\log d)/\log d\to1\), this gives the saving \(b/4\) in \hyperref[eq:31]{(31)}.

All coefficients of \(E_\sigma\) are nonnegative. The length in \(Y\) of its unscaled entry in \hyperref[eq:28]{(28)} is at most \((1+V)^T E_\sigma(U+T)\), by expanding \(E_\sigma(t+\partial_x)x^j\) and bounding powers of \(\partial_x\) on \(x^j\) by \(j^k(1+|x|)^j\). For integer \(u\ge0\), \(\binom{u+l}{l}\le [e(1+u/l)]^l\). For \(0<l<h\) use \(l\log(h/l)\le h/e\). It follows that \(\log E_\sigma(U+T)\le\sigma[1+\log(1+(U+T)/h)]+h\). By \hyperref[eq:29]{(29)}, after expanding the determinant, the logarithm per row is at most

\[
 \log n+(T/2)\log(2\operatorname{lcm}(1,\ldots,h))+O_d(1)
       +h+\bar\sigma[1+\log(1+(U+T)/h)] .
\]

For the length estimate expand \[
 e^{-tx}E_\sigma(\partial_x)(x^je^{tx})
   =\sum_{r=0}^j E_\sigma^{(r)}(t)(j)_{\underline r}\,x^{j-r}/r! .
\] Nonnegative coefficients of \(E_\sigma\) give \(|E_\sigma^{(r)}(t)|\le E_\sigma^{(r)}(U)\). Since \((j)_{\underline r}\le T^r\) and \(x=ivY/2\), the sum of coefficient moduli is at most \((1+V)^T E_\sigma(U+T)\) by Taylor expansion. For an integer \(u\ge0\), \(B_l(u)\le\binom{u+l}l\le[e(1+u/l)]^l\) follows from \(l!\ge(l/e)^l\) by integral comparison for \(\log(l!)\). Full factors of size \(h\) cost \(h[1+\log(1+u/h)]\) each. A remaining factor of size \(0<l<h\) costs at most \(l[1+\log(1+u/h)]+l\log(h/l)\), using \(1+u/l\le(h/l)(1+u/h)\). Maximizing \(x\log(1/x)\) gives at most \(1/e\) for \(0<x\le1\), so \(h\) covers this one extra cost. Take \(u=U+T\). In each permutation all row sizes occur once and \(\sum j=nT/2\), so multiplying by the \(d_j\) produces exactly the average logarithmic cost in the display. The number of permutations is at most \(n^n\).

The log lcm is \(O(h)\) by the prime number theorem, since prime powers of exponent at least two contribute \(O(\sqrt h\log^2 h)\). As \(\log(1+b\mathscr D)/\log d\to1\), multiplication by \((d-1)/(\mathscr D X)\) leaves at most \(s/2+o_*(1)\). Finally \(T/(2\mathscr D)\to a/2\) and \(l_0/(n\mathscr D)\ge ab/(4s)-o_*(1)\). Formula \hyperref[eq:30]{(30)} now reads

\[
 \frac{\log|R^0(\alpha)|}{n\mathscr D X}\ge -D_1-o_*(1),\qquad
 D_1=\frac a2+\frac s2-\frac b4-\frac{ab}{4s}.                            \tag{31}\label{eq:31}
\]

To track these scales, \(\log L_h=\sum_{p^j\le h}\log p=\vartheta(h)+O(\sqrt h\log^2 h)\), counting \(j\ge2\) trivially using \(p\le\sqrt h\) and \(j\le\log_2h\). Since \(T=O(\mathscr D)\) and \(h\le X/\mathscr D\), the term \(T\log L_h=O(X)\) disappears after multiplication by \(d/(\mathscr D X)\) in the successive limits. The term with \(\bar\sigma\) contributes at most \((s/2+o_*(1))(1+\log(1+b\mathscr D))/\log d=s/2+o_*(1)\). Terms \(\log n+O_d(1)\) disappear already in the height limit. Finally \(n/(\mathscr D X)\to 2ab\) in the successive limits, so \(l_0/(n\mathscr D)\ge n/[8(S+1)\mathscr D]-1/(2\mathscr D)=ab/(4s)-o_*(1)\). Thus the losses in \hyperref[eq:31]{(31)} come from half the layer height and the mean row order. The two gains come from \(K\) and the removed power at the origin.

\subsection{B.3. Analytic upper bound}\label{b.3.-analytic-upper-bound}

Fix \(z=4/39\), \(t_0=9999/10000\), and let \(p_{t_0}\) be the path of Section 6. At \(w=z\), split each column in \hyperref[eq:28]{(28)} into its value at \(\pi\) and its difference between \(\alpha\) and \(\pi\). Continue the former by the entries \([E_\sigma(\partial_x)(x^j e^{tx})]_{x=(i\pi/2)v p_{t_0}(w)}\). At \(w=z\) these reproduce the original phases. Keep the difference columns at the actual point. Each carries one factor \(\alpha-\pi\), since \(\partial_x(\partial_t+x)^j=j(\partial_t+x)^{j-1}\) as an operator derivative. The remaining polynomial parts obey the bound used in B.2 times a constant depending on \(d\), as \(|\alpha-\pi|\le1\).

In this continuation take out the scalar \(d_j\) from each whole column, accounting for their product as in B.2. At the point \(w=z\), decompose the remaining column as its \(\pi\) value plus the difference, retaining exactly the same selected rows. In polynomial factors the difference equals the integral of the derivative along the straight segment from \(\pi\) to \(\alpha\). Differentiating \((\partial_t+ivY/2)^j\) with respect to \(Y\) multiplies by \(jiv/2\) and lowers the power by one. The phase \(i^{vt}\) is fixed at both endpoints. On the bounded segment this gives one factor \(|\alpha-\pi|\) times \(E_\sigma(U+T)\) times a constant depending only on \(d\). Only the \(\pi\) columns are continued. A Laplace expansion along those columns pairs an analytic \(k\) by \(k\) minor with an error minor at the fixed point. The row sets are complements and there are at most \(2^n\) label choices and \(2^n\) row choices.

For an analytic minor expand \(x^j e^{tx}\) in powers \(x^m\). In column vectors the fixed operators \(E_\sigma(\partial_x)\) are the same for each occurrence of a given power. Repetition of \(m\) forces the determinant to vanish. Each derivative of order at most \(S\) lowers the power by at most \(S\), so \(\binom k2-kS\) is a valid lower bound for its zero order. This argument applies to every selected set of rows. On the boundary, termwise in a permutation, bound the exponential by \(\exp((\pi/2)|\operatorname{Im}p_{t_0}|\sum|tv|)\). The polynomial factors are bounded by \(E_\sigma(U+T)\) times a \(d\)-dependent factor since \(p_{t_0}\) is bounded on the circle and \(T,V\) are fixed in this height limit.

By multilinearity followed by Laplace expansion one gets a \(k\)-column analytic minor and an \((n-k)\)-column error minor. The analytic minor has origin order at least \(\binom k2-kS\) by the distinct-powers argument of \hyperref[eq:17]{(17)}. The argument permits arbitrary selected derivative rows and any row operator of degree at most \(S\). The number of minor choices and permutations contributes \(O(n\log n)\) to the logarithm. For its boundary exponent sort the \(k\) absolute frequencies and node indices in decreasing order. A given absolute frequency occurs at most \(2(T+1)\) times, and an absolute node at most \(2(S+1)\) times. The \(j\)th entries are bounded by \(U-(j-1)/(2(T+1))+1\) and \(V-(j-1)/(2(S+1))+1\). Pairing the two descending bounds maximizes the product sum. With \(x=k/n\), \(\eta=sc/(ab)>1\), this gives

\[
 \frac1{nUV}\sum_{\rm matched}|t v|\le J_\eta(x)+o_*(1),\qquad
 J_\eta(x)=\int_0^x(1-y)(1-y/\eta)\,dy
 =x-\tfrac12(1+1/\eta)x^2+\tfrac{x^3}{3\eta}.                            \tag{32}\label{eq:32}
\]

To prove the rearrangement bound, if \(a\ge b\) and \(c\ge d'\) then \(ac+bd'-(ad'+bc)=(a-b)(c-d')\ge0\). Removing every inversion maximizes the sum by pairing the descending lists of absolute values. In an arbitrary subset of \(k\) columns, the \(j\)th largest \(|t|\) is at most \(U-\lfloor(j-1)/(2(T+1))\rfloor\), because at most \(2(T+1)\) columns occur at a fixed absolute frequency. At a fixed \(|v|\) there are at most \(2(S+1)\) rows, giving the other bound. The integer floors can be relaxed by adding one in each list. Divide the product bound at rank \(j\) by \(UV\) and put \(y=(j-1)/n\). Since \(n/(2(T+1)U)=1+1/(2U)\) and \(2V(S+1)/n=\eta+o_*(1)\), the factors are bounded by \(1-y+o_*(1)\) and \(1-y/\eta+o_*(1)\) uniformly. Their bounded products have the same uniform accuracy. Riemann sums up to any partial endpoint \(k/n\) approximate the integral uniformly: the integrand has a bounded derivative and the sum of all rectangle errors is \(O(1/n)\). Integrating the quadratic gives \hyperref[eq:32]{(32)}.

Indeed \(2V(S+1)/n=\eta+o_*(1)\), and the Riemann sum error is \(O(1/U+1/V+1/n)\) uniformly in \(k\). Section 6 gives \(B_*=\mathbb E_z|\operatorname{Im}p_{t_0}|\le(1-z^2)/(2z)+(z^{-1}+t_0z)(1-t_0)/(4t_0)\). Poisson's bound for the zero, \hyperref[eq:32]{(32)} for the exponential, and the \(n-k\) difference factors now yield

\[
 \frac{\log|R^0(\alpha)|}{n\mathscr D X}\le
 \max_{0\le x\le1}
 \{-C(1-x)-ab\log(1/z)x^2+(\pi/2)B_*bcJ_\eta(x)\}+o_*(1).               \tag{33}\label{eq:33}
\]

In this normalization \(\binom{k}{2}/(n\mathscr D X)=abx^2+o_*(1)\) and \(kS/(n\mathscr D X)=o_*(1)\). For the nonexponential part the per-row bound in B.2 with \(\sigma\le S\) is \(O(X(1+\log(1+b\mathscr D)))+O_d(1)\) after the height limit. The column cost is \(O(Th+T)\) per row. Dividing both by \(\mathscr D X\) makes them \(o_*(1)\), explaining their absence in \hyperref[eq:33]{(33)}.

In \hyperref[eq:33]{(33)} the \((n-k)\) error columns contribute at most \(-C(1-x)\) under the assumed closeness. The Poisson penalty for the zero is \(-\log(1/z)(\binom k2-kS)/(n\mathscr D X)\). Here \(n/(2\mathscr D X)=ab+o_*(1)\) and \(kS/(n\mathscr D X)\le S/(\mathscr D X)=o_*(1)\). The boundary exponential contributes \((\pi/2)B_*UV/(\mathscr D X)\) times \hyperref[eq:32]{(32)}, with \(UV/(\mathscr D X)=bc+o_*(1)\). The scalar estimate after \hyperref[eq:20]{(20)} gives \(B_*\le2A_z/\pi+(z^{-1}+t_0z)(1-t_0)/(4t_0)\), exactly the bound used here. The nonexponential row and column costs established above are uniform in \(k\). Taking the maximum over all \(x\in[0,1]\) covers \(k=0\) and \(k=n\) as well.

\subsection{B.4. A strict comparison}\label{b.4.-a-strict-comparison}

The exact enclosures of Appendix A give \(\log(39/4)>2.27726\), \(\pi/2<355/226\), \(B_*<4.824\). Put \(g_*=(355/226)(4.824)bc\). It suffices to bound the cubic below on the unit interval. Put

\[
 p(x)=C(1-x)+2.27726\,abx^2-g_*J_\eta(x)-D_1 .                           \tag{34}\label{eq:34}
\]

On each of 128 equal subintervals write \(p((h+t)/128)=\sum_{j=0}^3u_jt^j\). Its cubic Bernstein coefficients are \(b_i=\sum_{j=0}^iu_j\binom ij/\binom3j\). Since the basis functions \(\binom3i t^i(1-t)^{3-i}\) are nonnegative and sum to one, \(p\) is bounded below by the smallest coefficient. Over all 512 rational coefficients the minimum equals \(40243808891539/22705471488000000>1/1000\). The supplementary verifier recomputes it. The two successive upper limits in \(o_*(1)\) therefore leave a contradiction for every sufficiently large fixed \(d\) and then every sufficiently large \(X\).

Here is the basis conversion in the certificate. Put \(B_{i,3}(t)=\binom3i t^i(1-t)^{3-i}\). The binomial theorem gives \(\sum_i B_{i,3}=1\) and each is nonnegative on \([0,1]\). For \(0\le j\le3\), \[
 \sum_{i=j}^3\frac{\binom ij}{\binom3j}B_{i,3}(t)
 =t^j\sum_{i=j}^3\binom{3-j}{i-j}t^{i-j}(1-t)^{3-i}=t^j .
\] If \(p(x)=\sum_{k=0}^3P_kx^k\) in ordinary powers, the coefficient after changing variable to \(x=(h+t)/128\) is \(u_j=\sum_{k=j}^3P_k\binom kj(h/128)^{k-j}128^{-j}\). Applying the monomial identity yields the four \(b_i\) printed in the conversion rule, making each value on the cell a convex combination of those numbers. The minimum over the \(128\cdot4\) coefficients (attained at \(h=99\), \(i=1\), with both indices starting at zero) bounds \textbf{every point} of the interval. Because \(J_\eta(x)\ge0\) on \([0,1]\), the replacements for \(\pi/2,B_*\) weaken the gain and the lower replacement for \(\log(1/z)\) weakens the zero term, as required for a lower bound on \(p\). Consequently the maximum in \hyperref[eq:33]{(33)} is at most \(-D_1-1/1000\) before the vanishing errors. Taking the two successive-limit errors small enough (for example less than \(1/4000\) each) is incompatible with \hyperref[eq:31]{(31)}.

Finally a real principal root must satisfy \(c_1\le C d\log d\) on any sequence, by the preceding contradiction (a larger \(c_1\) would give the forbidden strict root inequality). By \hyperref[eq:4]{(4)}, \(\sum c_i-1\le C d\log d+d-2\). The nonreal alternative gives at most \(2d-3\). For any exponent strictly above \(C d\log d+d-2\) the endpoint contradiction and factorization argument of Section 7 now apply. This proves \hyperref[eq:2]{(2)} with the supremum definition of \(\omega_d\).

The final quantifiers matter. There exists \(d_0\) such that for every fixed \(d\ge d_0\) there is a height threshold beyond which a real root cannot have \(|\alpha-\pi|<e^{-Cd\log d\,X}\). When \(c_1>Cd\log d\) the bounded root size from Section 2 gives exactly this forbidden inequality eventually. For a nonreal principal root use \(2d-3\), which is smaller than \(Cd\log d+d-2\) for all sufficiently large \(d\). For any fixed \(w>Cd\log d+d-2\) these bounds exclude an irreducible sequence with limiting value exponent at least \(w\). For instance apply Section 7's uniform-constant argument at some \(w'\) strictly between the two, multiply the factors, and let \(H(P)\) grow. The resulting constant times \(H(P)^{-w'}\) eventually exceeds \(H(P)^{-w}\). This proves the stated bound on the supremum. Dividing by \(d\log d\) leaves \(C+(d-2)/(d\log d)\to208/25\). Appendix B asserts neither a numerical value for \(d_0\) nor a uniform constant at its displayed supremum bound.

\section{Appendix C. Two background theorems used in the proof}\label{appendix-c.-two-background-theorems-used-in-the-proof}

Section 2 takes the logarithm of \(|f(\pi)|\) even for a single fixed polynomial. Section 4 converts prime sums to ordinary integrals. This appendix proves the precise facts that justify these operations using polynomials, elementary complex analysis and integration. The statements have independent roles: qualitative transcendence alone gives no exponent in Theorem 1.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{C.1. Why an integer polynomial cannot vanish at \(\pi\)}{C.1. Why an integer polynomial cannot vanish at π}}\label{c.1.-why-an-integer-polynomial-cannot-vanish-at-pi}

Suppose to the contrary that \(\pi\) is algebraic. Then \(\alpha=i\pi\) is algebraic and \(1+e^\alpha=0\). Let \(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_m\) be its conjugates, including \(\alpha\). Expand the finite product \(\prod_{j=1}^m (1+e^{\alpha_j})\). A subset \(S\) of the indices contributes \(e^{\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_j}\). Let \(k\) count subsets with sum zero, including the empty set. List the nonzero sums as \(\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_s\), retaining multiplicities. Because one factor of the product vanishes, \[
 k+\sum_{\nu=1}^s e^{\beta_\nu}=0,\qquad k\ge1 .
\] Here \(s\ge1\), for example the subset containing just the index of \(\alpha\). The product of \(x-\sum_{j\in S}\alpha_j\) over all subsets has rational coefficients: each coefficient is a symmetric polynomial with integer coefficients in the \(\alpha_j\) and their elementary symmetric functions are rational by the defining polynomial. The elementary symmetric polynomial rule follows by subtracting multiples of products of elementary symmetric functions to eliminate the largest ordered monomial successively. Removing the \(k\) factors \(x\) therefore leaves \(\prod_\nu(x-\beta_\nu)\) in \(\mathbb Q[x]\). Choose an integer \(c\ge1\) clearing its denominators and put \(A(x)=c\prod_\nu(x-\beta_\nu)\in\mathbb Z[x]\). Then \(A(0)\ne0\).

We need one elementary integrality fact. An algebraic integer is a root of a monic polynomial in \(\mathbb Z[x]\). Each \(c\beta_\nu\) is one, by multiplying \(A(x/c)\) by \(c^{s-1}\). Sums and products of finitely many algebraic integers are algebraic integers: monic relations reduce all powers to an integer span of finitely many products of bounded powers, including \(1\). Multiplication by an element of the generated ring maps this span into itself and is represented on a finite generating list by an integer matrix. Its characteristic polynomial annihilates that multiplication on the span by Cayley--Hamilton, hence annihilates the element by evaluation at \(1\). A rational algebraic integer is an integer by substituting a reduced fraction in its monic equation.

Choose arbitrarily large primes \(p\) avoiding the fixed nonzero integer \(kcA(0)\). Put \[
 D=(s+1)p-1,\qquad H_p(x)=x^{p-1} A(x)^p,\qquad
 g_p(x)=\frac{c^D H_p(x)}{(p-1)!},\qquad
 F_p(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{D}g_p^{(j)}(x).
\] The scaling \(c^D\) clears the denominators from evaluating degree-\(D\) integer polynomials at any \(\beta_\nu\) in the ring of algebraic integers: for \(0\le r\le D\), \(c^D\beta_\nu^r=c^{D-r}(c\beta_\nu)^r\). At zero, terms of order \(j<p-1\) vanish, and the order-\(p-1\) derivative gives \(c^D A(0)^p\). For \(j\ge p\), the factor \(j!/(p-1)!\) is an integer multiple of \(p\), with the remaining coefficient integral. Thus \(F_p(0)\) is an integer congruent to \(c^D A(0)^p\) modulo \(p\). At each \(\beta_\nu\) the zero of \(A^p\) has order at least \(p\). For \(j\ge p\), \(H_p^{(j)}(x)/j!\) has integer coefficients by the binomial theorem. The same factor \(j!/(p-1)!\) and the scaling \(c^D\) show that \(F_p(\beta_\nu)/p\) is an algebraic integer. Their sum is rational because \(F_p\) has rational coefficients and the power sums of the multiset of \(\beta_\nu\) are rational symmetric functions. Hence \(\sum_\nu F_p(\beta_\nu)\) is an integer divisible by \(p\). In particular \[
 kF_p(0)+\sum_\nu F_p(\beta_\nu)
\] is an integer \textbf{not divisible by \(p\)}, and has absolute value at least one.

On the other hand \(F_p-F_p'=g_p\) by cancellation. Integration of \((e^{-x}F_p(x))'=-e^{-x}g_p(x)\) gives \[
 e^{\beta_\nu}\int_0^{\beta_\nu} e^{-x}g_p(x)\,dx
       =e^{\beta_\nu}F_p(0)-F_p(\beta_\nu).
\] Sum this over \(\nu\) along straight segments. The result is the negative of the nonzero integer above. The finitely many segments form a fixed compact set. There \(|x|\), \(|A(x)|\) and \(|e^{-x}|\) have fixed bounds, so the left side in absolute value is bounded by \(M^p/(p-1)!\) for a constant \(M\) independent of \(p\), absorbing the segment lengths, \(c^D\) and the fixed \(e^{\beta_\nu}\). This tends to zero because the ratio for successive integers \(p\) is \(M/p\). The contradiction proves that \(\pi\) is transcendental. In particular for any fixed finite set of nonzero input polynomials the minimum of the positive numbers \(H(P)^w |P(\pi)|\) is positive.

\subsection{C.2. The prime number theorem in the form needed for (9) and (31)}\label{c.2.-the-prime-number-theorem-in-the-form-needed-for-9-and-31}

We prove \(\vartheta(x)/x\to1\), where \(\vartheta(x)=\sum_{p\le x}\log p\) counts each prime once. This is the Chebyshev form of the prime number theorem. A source for the prime asymptotics is {[}3{]}. The contour argument below uses Newman's method {[}6{]}. No quantitative error term or distribution of primes in residue classes is needed.

\textbf{An initial bound.} For a positive integer \(n\), each prime \(n<p\le2n\) divides \(\binom{2n}n\): it occurs in the numerator factorial and neither denominator factorial. Thus \[
 \vartheta(2n)-\vartheta(n)\le\log\binom{2n}n\le 2n\log2 ,
\] the last inequality following from the sum of all binomial coefficients. Apply this with \(n=1,2,4,\ldots,2^{j-1}\) and sum to get \(\vartheta(2^j)\le2^{j+1}\log2\). Monotonicity bounds \(\vartheta(x)\) by a fixed multiple of \(x\) between consecutive powers of two. This bound is enough to control a Laplace transform.

\textbf{The analytic function detecting primes.} For \(\operatorname{Re}s>1\) define \(\zeta(s)=\sum_{n\ge1}n^{-s}\). Absolute convergence and unique factorization yield \(\zeta(s)=\prod_p(1-p^{-s})^{-1}\). The sum \(\sum_{p,k\ge1}p^{-ks}/k\) (with \(p\) prime) converges locally absolutely there, and its exponential equals this product. In particular \(\zeta\) has no zeros there. Also \[
 \zeta(s)=s\int_1^\infty \lfloor x\rfloor x^{-s-1}\,dx
 =\frac{s}{s-1}-s\int_1^\infty \{x\}x^{-s-1}\,dx .
\] The first identity integrates \(\sum_{n\le x}1\) term by term and the second uses \(\{x\}=x-\lfloor x\rfloor\). The last integral converges locally uniformly for \(\operatorname{Re}s>0\), including after differentiating on compact subsets. This continues \(\zeta\) with a simple pole of residue one at \(s=1\).

There is no zero on \(\operatorname{Re}s=1\) away from the pole. For \(\sigma>1\) and real \(\tau\ne0\) use \(3+4\cos u+\cos(2u)=2(1+\cos u)^2\ge0\) term by term in the real part of the Euler logarithm. Exponentiating gives \[
 \zeta(\sigma)^3 |\zeta(\sigma+i\tau)|^4 |\zeta(\sigma+2i\tau)|\ge1 .
\] If \(\zeta(1+i\tau)=0\), the middle factor is \(O((\sigma-1)^4)\) by analyticity. The first is \(O((\sigma-1)^{-3})\) by the pole, and the last is bounded by analyticity at \(1+2i\tau\). Their product tends to zero, a contradiction. Logarithmic differentiation of the Euler product gives \[
 P(s):=\sum_p(\log p)p^{-s}
 =-\frac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)}-\sum_p\sum_{k\ge2}(\log p)p^{-ks}.
\] The last sum is analytic for \(\operatorname{Re}s>1/2\) by absolute local convergence, comparing with \(\sum_{n\ge2}(\log n)n^{-2\sigma}\) times a geometric bound. Thus \(P(s)-1/(s-1)\) is analytic across each point of the line \(\operatorname{Re}s=1\). At \(1\) this follows by factoring the simple pole of \(\zeta\). A neighborhood of each finite segment suffices here.

\textbf{A boundary-value lemma for Laplace transforms.} Let \(f(t)\) be bounded and locally integrable on \([0,\infty)\), \(|f|\le M\). Its transform \(g(z)=\int_0^\infty f(t)e^{-zt}\,dt\) is analytic for \(\operatorname{Re}z>0\). If it extends analytically to a neighborhood of each point of the imaginary axis, then \(\int_0^T f(t)\,dt\to g(0)\). We give the contour proof because the behavior at the boundary is precisely what is needed.

Set \(g_T(z)=\int_0^T f(t)e^{-zt}\,dt\), an entire function. Fix \(R>0\) and let \(K_T(z)=e^{Tz}(1+z^2/R^2)/z\). Its residue at zero is one. Integrate \(g_TK_T\) around \(|z|=R\). For \(gK_T\) take the same right semicircle and close it just to the left of the imaginary axis inside the continuation region. For instance take the left half of a narrow ellipse from \(iR\) to \(-iR\). Compactness of the imaginary segment supplies room for it. Both positively oriented contours enclose zero. Their residue integrals give \(g_T(0)-g(0)\) by subtraction.

On the right arc, \(\sigma=\operatorname{Re}z>0\) gives \(|g_T-g|\le M e^{-T\sigma}/\sigma\). On the original left semicircle, \(\sigma<0\) gives \(|g_T|\le M(e^{-T\sigma}-1)/|\sigma|\). On either semicircle \(|1+z^2/R^2|=2|\sigma|/R\) and \(|z|=R\). Thus the absolute integrand of the difference on the right and of \(g_TK_T\) on the left is at most \(2M/R^2\). Their combined contribution after division by \(2\pi\) is at most \(2M/R\). On the short-leftward ellipse the integral of \(gK_T\) tends to zero: the fixed factors are bounded, the contour avoids zero, \(\operatorname{Re}z<0\) except at the endpoints, and \(e^{T\operatorname{Re}z}\le1\). Dominated convergence on the arc applies. It follows that \(\limsup_{T\to\infty}|g_T(0)-g(0)|\le 2M/R\). Since \(R\) was arbitrary, this proves the lemma.

\textbf{Removing the weight.} Set \(f(t)=e^{-t}\vartheta(e^t)-1\). It is bounded by the initial estimate. For \(\operatorname{Re}z>0\) integrate each prime contribution on \(t\ge\log p\) to get \[
 g(z)=\frac{P(1+z)}{1+z}-\frac1z .
\] The pole at zero cancels because \(P(1+z)=1/z+A(z)\) with \(A\) analytic near zero. The continuation just proved satisfies all hypotheses of the lemma. Therefore \(\int_0^T f\) converges, so every integral over a fixed-length interval whose endpoints tend to infinity tends to zero.

Fix \(\delta>0\) and write \(V(T)=e^{-T}\vartheta(e^T)\). Monotonicity of \(\vartheta\) gives \[
 \int_T^{T+\delta}(f(t)+1)\,dt\ge(1-e^{-\delta})V(T),\qquad
 \int_{T-\delta}^T(f(t)+1)\,dt\le(e^\delta-1)V(T).
\] Each integral tends to \(\delta\). Thus \(\limsup V(T)\le\delta/(1-e^{-\delta})\) and \(\liminf V(T)\ge\delta/(e^\delta-1)\). Let \(\delta\downarrow0\) to obtain \(V(T)\to1\). Substitution of \(T=\log x\) proves the theorem. Applying it to the interval masses in Section 4 and the partial summation in B.2 yields exactly the weighted prime limits used in the paper.

\section{Appendix D. Replaying the finite certificate}\label{appendix-d.-replaying-the-finite-certificate}

In a PDF reader, open the attachments panel and save both \textbf{pi-values-checker.zip} and \textbf{pi-values-case3.zip}. Together they contain the two original JSON witnesses, five readable Python modules, the dependency list and the original rational record. The same bytes are included in the LaTeX source. All mathematical deductions used to turn those data into a proof are in Sections 3--8 and Appendices A--B.

With Python 3.10 or later and NumPy installed, run the following from the directory containing both attachments, with assertions enabled. Both archives extract into the same directory.

\begin{verbatim}
python -m zipfile -e pi-values-checker.zip proof
python -m zipfile -e pi-values-case3.zip proof
python proof/verify.py --json fresh.json
\end{verbatim}

The analytical argument reduces to testing strict inequalities with rational inputs. The two case files supply the bands, weights, reference densities, charges, fields and angular mixtures. The geometry module computes \hyperref[eq:8]{(8)}, \hyperref[eq:9]{(9)} and the sorted moments. The energy module bounds \hyperref[eq:15]{(15)} on the full interval with \hyperref[eq:26]{(26)}. The interval and angular modules enclose \hyperref[eq:19]{(19)} and \hyperref[eq:22]{(22)}--\hyperref[eq:24]{(24)} with \hyperref[eq:27]{(27)}. The main program also recomputes every Bernstein coefficient in Appendix B. The archived record is a comparison file, never an input to the estimates.

A successful replay reports lower bounds \(192189/10^{10}\) and \(310983/10^{10}\) for the two strict gaps. For the large-degree test the minimum over all 512 coefficients is \(40243808891539/22705471488000000\). To compare all rational results, load the two JSON objects and check equality. This also checks the root thresholds, the direction slacks of each node and the endpoint exponents.

Appendix B & Independent \(a=33/8,b=3/5,c=6/7,s=511/125,C=208/25,z=4/39,t_0=9999/10000,\eta=sc/(ab)\). Minimum of 512 Bernstein coefficients \(40243808891539/22705471488000000>1/1000\). Height limit first, degree limit second. \\
\end{longtable}
}
}

Two notation points deserve care when comparing passages. The moment symmetry in Section 6.2 uses \(a_{-v}=a_v\), as holds in both inputs. The secondary quadratic parameter in Section 7 is denoted \(B^{\rm cost}\) here to distinguish it from the root size \(B_i\) in \hyperref[eq:5]{(5)}. In Appendix B.3 the column factors \(d_j\) are taken out before analytic continuation and charged to the height bound. This edition uses the two witnesses accompanying the 18 November manuscript throughout. The numerical check in Appendix D is a finite rational computation. The analytical steps and their quantifiers are given in the text.

\textbf{Edition note.} A later project checkpoint records \(16.161227\) and \(27.412216\) with a different pair of witnesses. \href{../../../research/pi_cross_constant_2026-11-18/CHECKPOINT.md}{Subsequent project checkpoint}.
